### [1] [Position: Reasoning is a Learnable Rule-Based Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12325)
*Rachel Lawrence,Jacqueline Maasch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that the AI community needs clear operational definitions for "reasoning" to ensure construct validity and measurable progress toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning, proposing definitions based on rule-based processes and a checklist for research communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is definitional ambiguity around "reasoning" in generative AI research, with the community often implicitly rejecting historical treatments from symbolic AI and logic. This ambiguity undermines construct validity of reasoning evaluation and makes quantifiable progress toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning difficult to verify.

Method: The paper provides (1) operational definitions for reasoning based on a synthesis of literature, positioning it as a learnable rule-based process grounded in concepts of valid and sound reasoning; and (2) a checklist for best practices in communicating AI reasoning research to promote clarity and verifiability.

Result: The paper contends that the definitional ambiguity is addressable through clear operational definitions and improved research communication practices, aiming to establish a more rigorous foundation for evaluating and advancing autonomous reasoning systems.

Conclusion: Clear operational definitions and standardized communication practices are essential for making verifiable progress toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning, bridging the gap between generative AI approaches and historical symbolic AI foundations in logic and verifiable automated reasoning.

Abstract: Autonomous reasoning is among the most scientifically and economically motivating topics in AI today. Historically the purview of symbolic AI, recent advances have mainly emerged from deep probabilistic generative models. Despite immense interest and rapid progress, the generative AI community has not clearly converged on operational definitions for reasoning and often implicitly rejects the historical treatment of this topic in logic and verifiable automated reasoning. This position contends that definitional ambiguity leaves the construct validity of reasoning evaluation unverifiable, undermining quantifiable progress toward trustworthy autonomous reasoning. We also contend that this ambiguity is addressable. To that end, we provide (1) operational definitions based on a synthesis of the literature, positioning valid and sound reasoning as a learnable rule-based process; and (2) a checklist for best practices in the communication of AI reasoning research.

</details>


### [2] [Diagnostic Foundation for Evaluating LLMs' Research Integrity as Co-Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12345)
*Yash Tripathi,Silu Sharma,Sai Sidhanth Manoharan Jayanthi,Shivank Garg,Lin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language models struggle with research integrity under institutional pressure, with 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions failing under peak pressure, creating risks of facilitating misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models are increasingly deployed as co-scientists, but their ability to maintain research integrity under institutional pressure hasn't been measured, creating concerns about potential misconduct and trust erosion in AI-assisted research.

Method: Created IntegrityBench benchmark with 36 paired tasks evaluating misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning, and artifact-grounded decision making under 5-level implicit-explicit pressure protocol across 3 domains and 4 research stages; tested 18 frontier model variants.

Result: Under peak pressure, models fail roughly 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions; explicit pressures induce compliance with misconduct while implicit contextual reframing causes over-refusal of legitimate tasks; correct ethical action doesn't require accurate classification; scale and reasoning ability don't reliably mitigate failures.

Conclusion: Frontier models can appear helpful while harboring integrity failures, creating two distinct deployment risks: facilitating research misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research, with these integrity facets being structurally dissociated.

Abstract: Language models are increasingly deployed as co-scientists, yet their ability to uphold research integrity under institutional pressure remains unmeasured. We introduce IntegrityBench, a benchmark evaluating misconduct classification, ethical action reasoning and artifact-grounded decision making across 36 paired tasks under a 5-level implicit-explicit pressure protocol spanning 3 domains and 4 research stages. Evaluating 18 frontier model variants, we find that under peak pressure, models fail roughly 1 in 3 integrity-critical decisions, and neither scale nor reasoning ability reliably mitigates this. Explicit pressures induce compliance with misconduct, while implicit contextual reframing more often causes over-refusal of legitimate research tasks. Interestingly, models failing to classify research requests accurately perform equally or better on artifact-grounded decision making (85.7 vs. 79.4), suggesting the three facets are structurally dissociated and correct ethical action does not require accurate classification. Frontier models can thus appear helpful while harbouring integrity failures that create two distinct deployment risks: facilitating research misconduct and eroding trust in AI-assisted research.

</details>


### [3] [Agent Behavioral Contracts II: Certifying Compositional Reliability Without Assuming Independence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12895)
*Varun Pratap Bhardwaj,Garima Singh,Arun Pratap Bhardwaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper empirically demonstrates that conditional-independence assumptions in multi-agent reliability analysis are routinely violated in practice, particularly when agents share the same model, leading to overestimated system reliability. The authors develop sound finite-sample certificates that don't require structural assumptions about dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent reliability analysis typically assumes conditional independence between components and multiplies their individual reliabilities to estimate system reliability. However, this assumption is rarely tested, and violations could lead to dangerously optimistic reliability estimates, especially in safety-critical systems where operators rely on these calculations.

Method: The authors conducted preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions in a two-agent handoff scenario using deterministic scoring. They tested dependence between co-failures and developed a finite-sample certification method using linear programming over joint distributions with Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson bounds around measured co-execution moments. They also created an anytime-valid certificate that maintains type-I error control under optional stopping.

Result: When two agents use the same model, they co-fail on 90.0% of missions where either fails (log OR 6.66, phi 0.916), showing strong positive dependence. Using different models reduces this dependence. The error systematically inflates joint failure probability above independence assumptions. Their moment-based certification method significantly improves reliability bounds - enriching from 10 to 14 moment functionals narrowed the identified interval by 85.7% and lifted the certified floor from 0.2455 to 0.4116.

Conclusion: The conditional-independence assumption in multi-agent reliability analysis is routinely violated in practice, especially when agents share the same model, leading to dangerously optimistic reliability estimates. Standard dependence modeling approaches can produce misleading certificates that worsen with more data. The proposed moment-based finite-sample certification provides sound, assumption-free reliability bounds that are practical and informative for real-world system certification.

Abstract: Compositional reliability bounds for multi-agent systems multiply component reliabilities, a step licensed by a conditional-independence assumption that is routinely stated and rarely tested. We test it. Two instances of one model, in a two-agent handoff, co-fail on 90.0% of the missions on which either fails (log OR 6.66, 95% CI [6.38, 7.00]; phi 0.916), in a preregistered evaluation of 18,000 missions scored by deterministic code with no LLM judge. Substituting a different model reduces the association in six of six contrasts; substituting a different vendor, model already different, does not -- a registered hypothesis reported as a null.
  The error is signed and runs against the operator: positive dependence inflates joint failure above the independence product, so redundancy is over-credited exactly when components share a model. The assumption-free alternative is often vacuous, and fitting a dependence model is worse: we prove a bootstrap bound on a fitted model's functional loses coverage of the truth as n grows, the identification gap being O(1) while the bootstrap haircut is O(n^{-1/2}). More data makes such a certificate worse, with no visible symptom.
  We give a finite-sample certificate assuming no dependence structure: a linear program over the joint, over a Bonferroni-Clopper-Pearson box around measured co-execution moments. It is sound, sharp for the information supplied, and monotone in the moment family. Enriching ten moment functionals to fourteen narrows the identified interval by 85.7% and lifts the certified floor from 0.2455 to 0.4116. A companion anytime-valid certificate holds type-I error at 0.0471 under optional stopping.
  Common dependence statistics are marginal-bounded and can reverse an apparent ordering of conditions when the compared agents fail at different rates. Contracts, scoring code, analysis scripts, and the preregistration are released.

</details>


### [4] [Position: The Alignment Community is Unintentionally Building a Censor's Toolkit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12346)
*Sarah Ball,Phil Hackemann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI alignment methods designed to prevent harm can be weaponized for censorship and manipulation, creating a dual-use risk that requires urgent discussion and mitigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by recognizing that AI alignment techniques, originally created to ensure AI safety and prevent harmful outputs, have a dangerous flip side: they can be repurposed as powerful tools for information control and manipulation by malicious actors.

Method: The authors analyze current alignment techniques by mapping them to potential and actual cases of misuse, demonstrating how these safety mechanisms can be turned into censorship tools, and examine contextual factors that exacerbate the risk.

Result: The analysis shows that the pursuit of perfectly aligned AI models inadvertently creates ever-improving tools for informational dominance, with risks amplified by rapid AI adoption, economic power asymmetries, and shifting political landscapes toward authoritarianism.

Conclusion: The AI community must urgently address the intentional misuse potential of alignment mechanisms and develop mitigation strategies to safeguard against this dual-use threat before it's too late.

Abstract: This position paper argues that modern AI alignment methods - originally designed to prevent harmful output - are dual-use technologies that may easily be misused by malicious actors for censorship and manipulation. By mapping current alignment techniques to the possibility and actual cases of misuse, we show that the quest for a "perfectly aligned" model inadvertently also provides malicious actors with an ever-improving tool for informational dominance. We need to discuss this dual-use potential now, as its risk is exacerbated by rapid user adoption of AI as information provider, economic power asymmetries, and a political landscape that increasingly shifts towards authoritarianism. We conclude by urging the community to consider the intentional misuse of AI alignment mechanisms and propose mitigation strategies to safeguard against this dual-use potential.

</details>


### [5] [OGR-MARL: Option-Guided Residual Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Heterogeneous USV Cooperative Pursuit in Constrained Port Waterways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12995)
*Mao Jiayang,Wang Lanfeng,Peng Zhao-Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OGR-MARL is a framework combining rule guidance with residual policy learning for heterogeneous USV pursuit in constrained port waterways, achieving high capture rates and good generalization without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous unmanned surface vehicle cooperative pursuit in constrained port waterways requires interception under multiple constraints (navigation, traffic, role constraints), making pure learning approaches inefficient due to complex constraint exploration.

Method: Proposes OGR-MARL framework that integrates: 1) shared evader belief, 2) role-conditioned option targets, 3) adaptive rule penalties, and 4) residual policy learning. The framework learns corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors rather than exploring from scratch. It's decoupled from specific MARL algorithms and instantiated with MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, and MASAC backbones.

Result: In Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario, OGR-MASAC achieves 75.0% capture rate, demonstrates promising mission-effective rule compliance, and shows best heterogeneous coordination. Zero-shot transfer to QGIS/AIS-informed map without retraining achieves promising results, showing strong generalization.

Conclusion: OGR-MARL effectively addresses heterogeneous USV cooperative pursuit in constrained environments by combining rule guidance with residual learning, demonstrating superior performance, better constraint handling, and strong generalization potential to more complex port scenarios.

Abstract: Heterogeneous USV cooperative pursuit in constrained port waterways requires evader interception under navigation, traffic, and role constraints. This paper proposes OGR-MARL, an option-guided residual multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that is decoupled from a specific MARL algorithm. OGR-MARL integrates shared evader belief, role-conditioned option targets, adaptive rule penalties, and residual policy learning, allowing different MARL algorithms to learn corrective actions on top of rule-guided behaviors rather than exploring constrained port environments from scratch. We instantiate OGR-MARL with representative continuous-control MARL backbones, including MADDPG, MATD3, MAPPO, and MASAC, yielding OGR-MADDPG, OGR-MATD3, OGR-MAPPO, and OGR-MASAC. Experiments in an abstract Xiazhimen port-waterway scenario show that the OGR-MASAC instantiation achieves a 75.0% capture rate, promising mission-effective rule compliance, and the best heterogeneous coordination among the tested methods. Without retraining, zero-shot transfer to a QGIS/AIS-informed Xiazhimen map achieves promising results, demonstrating the generalization potential of OGR-MARL in more complex port scenarios.

</details>


### [6] [Agreement Is Not Alignment: Divergent Moral Grounds in Human and LLM Ethical Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12368)
*Octavian M. Machidon,Alina L. Machidon,Vojko Strahovnik,Mateja Centa Strahovnik,Jonas Miklavčič,Marko Robnik Šikonja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TLDR: While LLMs often agree with human moral judgments on final labels, their rationales show systematic divergence in moral principles and priorities, revealing that label-based agreement is misleading and doesn't equal true moral alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the common practice of using agreement with human judgments as a proxy for evaluating LLM moral alignment. The authors recognize that two agents can reach the same final judgment while appealing to completely different moral principles, contextual assumptions, or situational interpretations, suggesting that label-based agreement may be insufficient for assessing true moral alignment.

Method: The researchers created a curated 500-item benchmark derived from ETHICS, spanning five domains of moral judgment. They collected new annotations from both human annotators and LLMs that include both final labels and supporting rationales. They analyzed frontier and open model families, comparing agreement at both the label level and the rationale level to identify systematic differences in moral reasoning.

Result: Across various LLM families, agreement with human annotator majority labels was often high. However, rationale-level analysis revealed systematic divergence in the moral grounds expressed by humans versus models. Models showed different distributions of attention across moral categories like harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, and excuse relevance, even when their final labels matched human majorities.

Conclusion: Agreement with human labels should not be treated as equivalent to moral alignment. Label-based evaluation can be misleadingly reassuring unless complemented by analysis of the underlying reasons, principles, and moral priorities expressed in model judgments. The research highlights the importance of examining rationales, not just outcomes, when assessing LLM moral reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Agreement with human judgments is a common proxy for evaluating the alignment of large language models (LLMs). Yet agreement in final labels does not show that human annotators and models rely on the same moral grounds. Two agents may reach the same judgment while appealing to different principles, contextual assumptions, or interpretations of the situation.
  We test this distinction using a curated 500-item ETHICS-derived benchmark spanning five domains of moral judgment, with new human annotator and LLM annotations of both final labels and supporting rationales. Across frontier and open model families, agreement with human annotator majority labels is often high. However, rationale-level analysis reveals systematic divergence in the moral grounds expressed by human annotators and models. In particular, models redistribute attention across categories such as harm, respect, promise-keeping, justice, desert, and excuse relevance, even when their final labels match the human annotator majority.
  Our results show that agreement should not be treated as equivalent to alignment. Label-based evaluation can therefore be misleadingly reassuring unless complemented by analysis of the reasons, principles, and moral priorities expressed in model judgments.

</details>


### [7] [Multi-Agent Scheduling with LLM-Assisted Contract Net Negotiation for Stream Processing in Mobile Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12371)
*Sabeur Lajili,Zaki Brahmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MAS-DecStream with LLM-MR-CNP, an enhanced Contract Net Protocol for stream processing in edge-cloud environments, using multi-round LLM-assisted negotiations to improve scheduling decisions and reduce QoS violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stream processing systems face challenges in heterogeneous mobile edge-cloud infrastructures due to workload volatility, resource contention, and strict QoS requirements, which complicate decentralized scheduling.

Method: LLM-MR-CNP extends the classical Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. Edge-cluster agents use LLMs to refine natural-language offloading proposals based on local observations, predicted resource states, and runtime context.

Result: Experiments using Alibaba ASI Trace show MAS-DecStream reduces latency violations to 3%, eliminates resource overcommitment, achieves 0.91 conflict-resolution rate with 20 agents, and improves utility by up to 22% over multi-round rule-based baselines.

Conclusion: Multi-round CNP refinement is the primary protocol-level improvement, with LLM assistance adding value for handling qualitative and uncertain runtime context, though accuracy-cost trade-offs depend on models and prompts.

Abstract: Stream-processing systems increasingly operate across heterogeneous mobile edge--cloud infrastructures, where workload volatility, resource contention, and stringent quality-of-service (QoS) requirements complicate decentralized scheduling. This paper proposes \emph{MAS-DecStream}, whose main contribution is \emph{LLM-MR-CNP}: an extension of the classical Contract Net Protocol with semantic CFP formulation, progressive context disclosure, multi-round proposal revision, negotiation memory, and deterministic validation. Edge-cluster agents refine natural-language offloading proposals from local observations, predicted resource states, and qualitative runtime context, while hard resource and QoS constraints remain deterministic. Experiments derived from the Alibaba ASI Trace evaluate the extension at three levels: single- versus multi-round CNP, rule-based versus LLM-assisted refinement, and fixed-model single- versus multi-round negotiation. Under the evaluated configurations, MAS-DecStream reduces latency violations to 3\%, eliminates resource overcommitment, reaches a conflict-resolution rate of 0.91 with 20 agents, and improves utility by up to 22\% over the multi-round rule-based baseline. A separate 25-case evaluation shows model- and prompt-dependent accuracy--cost trade-offs. The results provide initial evidence that multi-round CNP refinement is the principal protocol-level gain, with LLM assistance adding value for qualitative and uncertain runtime context.

</details>


### [8] [Position: We Need Practical AI Alignment Methods to Mirror Human Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12372)
*Vijay Keswani,Breanna K. Nguyen,Cyrus Cousins,Vincent Conitzer,Walter Sinnott-Armstrong,Jana Schaich Borg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This position paper argues for cognitively-aligned AI systems that reason similarly to humans in high-stakes decision-making contexts, highlighting cognitive alignment as crucial for adoption and trustworthy AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems increasingly serve as decision aids, delegates, or autonomous decision-makers, there's a growing need for systems that reason similarly to humans in high-stakes situations. Cognitive misalignment represents a potential barrier to AI adoption, especially where users need to understand and trust AI rationales.

Method: The paper takes a position paper approach, reviewing existing evidence about cognitive alignment benefits, providing new survey data on user perceptions, analyzing gaps between current alignment methods and needed capabilities, and outlining a research agenda.

Result: Survey data shows many users find cognitive alignment "essential" when an AI's rationale matters. The analysis identifies gaps in existing alignment methods and proposes a research agenda to achieve better cognitive alignment. Evidence suggests cognitive alignment improves understandability and trustworthiness.

Conclusion: Cognitive alignment is critical for AI adoption in high-stakes applications where users need to understand and trust AI reasoning. Addressing cognitive misalignment is necessary for creating AI systems that users are both willing and justified to rely on, requiring focused research to bridge current gaps.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly employed as decision aids, decision delegates, or autonomous decision-makers. This position paper argues that in many settings, particularly high-stakes decision-making, we need accurate cognitively-aligned AI systems that reason similarly to their users, and faithfully communicate their reasoning. We review evidence that cognitive alignment improves understandability and trustworthiness, and provide new survey data showing that many users find cognitive alignment "essential" when an AI's rationale for a judgment or action is important to them. We outline the gaps between existing alignment methods and what is needed to achieve cognitive alignment, and present a research agenda to address these gaps. We argue that cognitive misalignment represents a likely impediment to AI adoption in many envisioned applications, and that addressing it is important for creating AI systems on which users are both willing and justified to rely.

</details>


### [9] [Don't Want Your LLM to Recommend Nuclear Strike? Try Asking It in Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12373)
*Rian Touchent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM safety alignment is language-dependent - some models show dramatically different nuclear launch decision rates in Japanese vs English prompts due to spontaneous moral reasoning, revealing that English-only safety evaluation misses critical risks and safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM safety alignment is typically evaluated only in English, creating blind spots for models used globally in high-stakes strategic contexts where language differences might affect model behavior and safety outcomes.

Method: Researchers tested 9 models across 6 providers using single-turn game-theoretic vignettes where models advise a nuclear-armed nation on whether to strike a defenseless opponent. They used strategically identical but amoral prompts across languages (particularly Japanese vs English) and conducted cross-language experiments to isolate mechanisms.

Result: Japanese prompts dramatically reduced launch rates in Claude models (from 40% to 0% in unnecessary scenarios, 93% to 17% in contested scenarios) and Gemini Pro 3.1 (53% to 13%). The effect comes from the reasoning language, not input language - when instructed to reason in Japanese with English prompts, launch rates dropped from 93% to 37%. Models reasoning in Japanese spontaneously generated moral vocabulary absent from prompts.

Conclusion: LLM safety behavior is language-dependent, with some models exhibiting significantly different risk profiles in different languages. English-only safety evaluation fails to capture both risks and safeguards embedded in other languages, requiring multilingual safety assessments for high-stakes applications.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used in strategic and advisory contexts, yet their safety alignment is typically evaluated in English only. We test nine models from six providers and ask whether the language of a prompt can change a model's decision in a high-stakes scenario. We use single-turn game-theoretic vignettes in which a model advises a nuclear-armed nation on whether to strike a defenseless opponent. The prompt is intentionally amoral and strategically identical across languages. We find that Japanese prompts reduce launch rates in the Claude model family: Claude Sonnet 4.6 drops from 40% to 0% in scenarios where the strike is unnecessary and from 93% to 17% in contested scenarios, with minimal effect when the strike is strategically rational. The effect extends to Gemini Pro 3.1 (53% to 13%). A cross-language experiment isolates the mechanism: when instructed to reason in Japanese in an English prompt, launch rates drop from 93% to 37%. It is the language the model is asked to reason in, not the language of the input, that drives the effect. When reasoning in Japanese, models spontaneously generate moral vocabulary (''moral cost'', ''millions of lives'') that is entirely absent from the prompt. Five other models show no language effect, but they launch in nearly every condition regardless of language. The effect requires a model that already hesitates in English. These results show that LLM safety behavior is language-dependent, and that evaluating in English alone can miss both risks and safeguards encoded in other languages.

</details>


### [10] [Dual-Flow Transformers: Decoupling the Primary Prefill Path from Additional Decode Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12385)
*Liming Liu,Mingze Wang,Tuo Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dual-Flow Transformer introduces a two-flow architecture where primary flow handles prompt processing and writes KV cache, while auxiliary flow activates only during token generation to add continuation-prediction computation without extra persistent state, enabling phase-specific optimization for inference efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As cumulative inference cost becomes increasingly important for large language models, there's a need to address the different hardware demands of prompt prefill (parallel, compute-bound) vs autoregressive decode (sequential, memory-bandwidth-bound). Conventional scaling increases both costs together, creating inefficiency in the inference pipeline.

Method: Dual-Flow Transformer separates computation into primary and auxiliary flows. Primary flow is a complete causal LM that processes prompts and writes KV cache. Auxiliary flow activates only from final prompt position onward, adding continuation-prediction computation without writing persistent state. Flows share major attention, MLP, and output matrices while using separate token embeddings and lightweight coupling.

Result: Dual-Flow achieves lower validation loss across architectures and data configurations in matched-token comparisons. For MoE models, the separation allows independent control over prompt cost, continuation cost, and predictive quality. Experiments show a prefill-decode-quality trade-off and demonstrate phase-specific expert allocation potential.

Conclusion: Dual-Flow Transformer enables phase-specific computation allocation, allowing optimization for both prompt prefill and token generation phases separately. This approach creates opportunities for efficient weight reuse and independent control over different inference phases, particularly beneficial for MoE models where expert allocation can be phase-specific.

Abstract: As large language models serve more requests, cumulative inference cost is becoming increasingly important relative to one-time training cost. The two inference phases stress hardware differently: prompt prefill is parallel and typically compute-bound, whereas autoregressive decode is sequential and often memory-bandwidth-bound. Conventional width or depth scaling increases both costs together because every added layer is evaluated in both phases. We ask whether additional learned computation can instead be allocated to continuation prediction while preserving the prompt-wide primary computation and a single persistent key-value (KV) cache. We introduce the Dual-Flow Transformer. Its primary flow is a complete causal language model that processes the prompt and writes the KV cache. The auxiliary flow is omitted during prompt processing and activated only from the final prompt position onward, adding continuation-prediction computation without writing persistent state or influencing the primary flow. The two flows share major attention, MLP, and output matrices, while using separate token embeddings and lightweight coupling. Sharing weights and the primary cache also creates opportunities to reuse loaded weights and cached keys and values during grouped execution. Across matched-token comparisons, Dual-Flow achieves lower validation loss across architectures and data configurations. In MoE models, the separation makes primary and auxiliary expert fan-outs independent controls over prompt cost, continuation cost, and predictive quality. We study two regimes: increasing decode computation at fixed prefill expert computation, and reallocating a fixed decode expert budget between the two flows. These experiments expose a prefill-decode-quality trade-off and demonstrate the potential of phase-specific expert allocation.

</details>


### [11] [Learning to Adapt Cross-Domain Preferences via Meta-LoRA for LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12389)
*Xuefei Wang,Jun Han,Zixuan Wang,Qingkai Zeng,Xiao Wang,Ruijie Wang,Jianxin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Meta-LoRA method for cross-domain personalization that combines PAC-Bayes regularization with functional decomposition of user and domain preferences to enable calibrated adaptation from few interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for cross-domain zero/few-shot personalization have two main problems: adaptation methods overfit when evidence is sparse, and history-transfer methods entangle user preferences with source-domain artifacts, causing negative transfer and unreliable priors.

Method: Proposes PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA: 1) Uses meta-learned LoRA initialization as both adaptation start point and prior center, 2) Adjusts update strength based on support-set size and predictive uncertainty to prevent overfitting, 3) Functionally decomposes personalization priors into user component (human-readable prompt for stable preferences) and domain component (topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific hidden-space conditioning).

Result: Experiments across multiple benchmarks show consistent gains: On HiCUPID, reduces cross-domain win-rate degradation by 47.9% relative to best competing baseline, and improves win rate by 110.2% under unseen-user cold start scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed approach effectively addresses the challenges of cross-domain personalization by calibrating adaptation to evidence quality while separating user and domain preferences, enabling more reliable transfer and preventing negative transfer artifacts from source domains.

Abstract: Cross-domain zero- or few-shot personalization aims to generate user-preferred responses in unseen conversational domains from only a handful of target-domain interactions. Existing adaptation methods struggle to calibrate update magnitude under sparse evidence and thus overfit, whereas history-transfer methods often entangle user preferences with source-domain artifacts, yielding unreliable personalization priors and negative transfer. To calibrate adaptation to evidence quality, we propose PAC-Bayes-regularized Meta-LoRA, which uses a meta-learned LoRA initialization as both the adaptation start and prior center, while adjusting update strength according to support-set size and predictive uncertainty. This limits overfitting under sparse or ambiguous evidence while permitting stronger personalization as evidence grows. Controlled adaptation alone does not determine which preferences should transfer across domains or how they should be expressed. We therefore functionally decompose personalization priors into user and domain components, using a human-readable prompt for stable preferences and topology-preserving soft tokens for domain-specific hidden-space conditioning. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and personalization tasks show consistent gains over strong baselines. On HiCUPID, our method reduces cross-domain win-rate degradation by 47.9% relative to the best competing baseline and improves win rate by 110.2% under unseen-user cold start.

</details>


### [12] [Research Assistant: AstraZeneca's Agentic System for R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12395)
*Piotr Grabowski,Mohamed Alameen,Jorge Bretones,Sabina Cardell,Miguel Carmona,Gavin Edwards,Ben Grainger,Sameh Hassan,Erik Jansson,Artur Kuziakhmetov,Albert Maristany,Hebatallah Mohamed,Andriy Nikolov,Sebastian Nilsson,Mark O'Donoghue,James Pacileo,Ashiq Sultan,Alex Voegele,Michael Ughetto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AstraZeneca developed an internal LLM-based Research Assistant system that provides chat-style access to diverse biomedical data sources, offering both fast Q&A and multi-step research capabilities with evidence-grounded responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientists and clinicians need to explore biomedical questions across numerous complex data sources (literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, etc.), which requires time-consuming manual searching and synthesis. An intelligent system could streamline R&D workflows by providing unified access to all relevant evidence.

Method: Built an internal LLM-based system with chat interface that retrieves evidence from multiple data sources (scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, internal systems). Features two modes: fast mode for direct Q&A and multi-step mode for complex research tasks. Responses are grounded in retrieved evidence with source linking.

Result: Successfully deployed at scale across AstraZeneca to support day-to-day R&D workflows. The system provides researchers with efficient access to diverse biomedical evidence through an intuitive chat interface, enabling faster exploration of scientific questions with traceable evidence.

Conclusion: LLM-based systems like Research Assistant can effectively support biomedical R&D by providing unified access to diverse data sources through intuitive interfaces. Key lessons include the importance of evidence grounding, source linking, and flexible interaction modes for different research needs.

Abstract: We describe Research Assistant, an internal LLM-based system developed at AstraZeneca to help scientists and clinicians explore biomedical questions across a broad range of data sources. The system provides a chat-style interface that brings together evidence from scientific literature, knowledge graphs, chemistry, clinical trials, safety resources, expression data, and internal experimental systems. It supports both a fast mode for direct question answering and a multi-step mode for more complex research tasks. Responses are grounded in retrieved evidence and linked back to the original sources, allowing users to review and further explore the underlying data. In this technical note, we outline the system architecture, the main design choices behind the product, and lessons learned from deploying it at scale to support day-to-day R&D workflows across AstraZeneca.

</details>


### [13] [Large Language Models Can Follow Instructions, But Not Many at Once: Phase Transitions in Compositional Constraint Satisfaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12426)
*Mariya I. Vasileva*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs struggle with multiple simultaneous constraints - performance collapses multiplicatively beyond 5-6 constraints, with structural constraints degrading twice as fast as lexical ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models need to handle multiple constraints simultaneously in real-world deployments, but performance degradation in compositional settings remains poorly understood and systematically uncharacterized.

Method: Introduced Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a procedurally generated benchmark systematically varying number of simultaneous constraints (k=1-12), with rule-based verifier scoring. Evaluated 15 models across 36 constraint types with 369,753 checks and zero LLM-judge involvement.

Result: 1) Individual constraint pass rates decay gradually (~41% at k=8), but all-constraint success collapses multiplicatively (5.7% for same scenario). 2) Structural constraints degrade 2x faster than lexical ones. 3) Failures are nearly independent, with residual coupling tracking shared output features rather than pairwise interference. Probe-level success falls below 50% at 7 constraints for strongest model.

Conclusion: Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5-6 simultaneous constraints, revealing fundamental limitations in LLMs' compositional constraint-handling capabilities that need addressing for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in settings that require simultaneous adherence to multiple explicit constraints - reasoning structure, safety boundaries, output schemas. Individual constraints are handled proficiently, but the compositional regime, where many must hold jointly, remains poorly characterized: how rapidly does performance degrade, what governs the degradation, and can the collapse be mitigated? We introduce Constraint Saturation Evaluation (CSE), a procedurally generated benchmark that systematically varies the number of simultaneous constraints (k), with every constraint scored by a deterministic, rule-based verifier and zero LLM-judge involvement: 15 models, 36 constraint types, 369,753 checks at k=1-12. Three findings emerge. First, per-constraint pass rate decays gradually and predictably, while the chance of satisfying all k constraints collapses - a model passing individual constraints at ~41% at k=8 succeeds on all eight just 5.7% of the time. Second, constraints do not degrade equally: structural constraints lose 2x more baseline capability per added constraint than lexical ones, ordered by a comprehension-maintenance gap that separates constraints requiring sustained tracking from binary decisions immune to composition. Third, failures are nearly independent, which is what makes the accumulation multiplicative; the residual coupling that does exist tracks shared output features rather than pairwise interference - a wrong sentence count fails every constraint that reads it. Reliable instruction following breaks down beyond 5-6 simultaneous constraints: probe-level success falls below 50% at 7 constraints for the strongest model, and at 3 or fewer for 12 of 15.

</details>


### [14] [MindMemOS: A Portable and Self-Evolving Memory Operating Layer for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12428)
*Kaichao Liang,Yuqi Cui,Hao Kong,Xinyuan Huang,Guohaotian Hou,Qingcan Kang,Liang Chen,Yiyang Yin,Ke Ye,Jiaquan Guo,Da Chen,Lingan Zeng,Yixing Peng,Rong Yao,Shixiong Kai,Mingxuan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MindMemOS is a self-evolving memory operating layer for AI agents that organizes open-world information and continuously evolves memory schemas and skills through evolutionary algorithms and autonomous refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory systems for AI agents are often fixed after development, limiting their ability to adapt memory models, organization strategies, and procedural knowledge through continued use. There's a need for memory systems that can evolve and improve over time.

Method: MindMemOS uses a unified entity-property-time structure to organize open-world information. It employs MindMemEvolve algorithm with validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios. The system includes 'whiledreaming' for memory consolidation (merging redundant records, resolving conflicts) and uses implicit corrective feedback as human-in-the-loop signals. MindSkillEvolve transforms agent execution trajectories into reusable, progressively refined skills.

Result: MindMemOS achieves 94.03% accuracy on LOCOMO and 70.63% on PersonaMem benchmarks. MindSkillEvolve improves SpreadsheetBench success rate by 9.2 percentage points over the initial-skill baseline.

Conclusion: MindMemOS demonstrates that portable, self-evolving memory systems can significantly improve AI agent performance across various tasks by enabling continuous adaptation of memory organization and skill development through evolutionary optimization and autonomous refinement.

Abstract: Memory is a core component of AI agents, enabling them to accumulate experience, maintain personalization, and adapt over long-term interactions. However, existing memory systems often remain fixed after development, limiting their ability to adapt their memory models, organization strategies, and procedural knowledge through continued use. We present MindMemOS, a portable and self-evolving memory operating layer that organizes open-world information using a unified entity property timestructure. MindMemOS supports scenario-adaptive memory modeling, higher-order pattern discovery, autonomous memory refinement, and continuous skill evolution. Its MindMemEvolve algorithm employs validation-driven evolutionary search to optimize memory schemas for target scenarios, whiledreaming consolidates accumulated memories by merging redundant records and resolving conflicts. In addition, implicit corrective feedback serves as a human-in-the-loop signal for identifying and revising potentially inaccurate or misaligned memories. Its MindSkillEvolve algorithm further transforms agent execution trajectories into reusable and progressively refined skills. MindMemOS achieves 94.03% accuracy on LOCOMO and 70.63% on PersonaMem. MindSkillEvolve improves SpreadsheetBench success by 9.2 percentage points over the initial-skill baseline.

</details>


### [15] [Governed Persistent Memory: Source-Bound State Semantics and Fail-Closed Release for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12476)
*Guodong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GPM introduces an auditable bitemporal state-transition model for agent memory governance, ensuring integrity through five executable clauses, with strong experimental validation showing near-perfect correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional agent memory systems (select-store-retrieve) lack governance mechanisms to handle contradictory, superseded, retracted, deleted, or stale records when making claims, creating reliability and auditability issues.

Method: Governed Persistent Memory (GPM) - an auditable bitemporal state-transition model with source-bound admission, derived lifecycle state, current public barriers, and fail-closed structured release. Five executable clauses ensure ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction/deletion, and exact claim closure.

Result: On GPM-ReleaseBench (3,600 cases), GPM matches all complete outcomes. Governed lane achieves 2,400/2,400 correct clusters vs 600/2,400 for ungoverned baseline, repairing all 1,800 baseline failures. Finite model explores 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states without counterexamples. Differential testing yields zero mismatches across 100,000 traces.

Conclusion: GPM provides a robust governance framework for agent memory that ensures deterministic, auditable outputs, significantly improving correctness over ungoverned approaches, though results are bounded to contractual and implementation verification rather than open-world truth claims.

Abstract: Long-term agent memory is usually treated as select--store--retrieve, but retrieval does not decide whether contradictory, superseded, retracted, deleted, or stale records may support an outgoing claim. We introduce Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), an auditable bitemporal state-transition model with source-bound admission, derived lifecycle state, current public barriers, and fail-closed structured release. Five executable clauses cover ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction or deletion, and exact claim closure over a fresh view at one verified head.
  On a prespecified hash-frozen 3,600-case GPM-ReleaseBench, GPM matches all complete outcomes; the strongest of three intentionally simple complete policies matches 1,800/3,600 and makes unmatched releases on 50% of violation cases. A separate sealed end-to-end service evaluation exercises real ingestion and release across eight query families. In its publicly disclosed V3 arm, the governed lane is correct on 2,400/2,400 clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungoverned local Qwen2.5-7B; it repairs all 1,800 baseline failures with no regression (one-sided 95% lower bounds 99.875% and 99.834%). A later V5 reseal over Chinese- and English-command arms, with generation-date pinning and no post-freeze reducer amendment, again obtains 2,400/2,400 per arm. A production-code-independent finite model explores 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states without a full-contract counterexample, and a 100,000-trace three-engine differential yields zero mismatches.
  These are bounded contract and implementation results, not open-world model accuracy or evidence of world truth. Governed answers in the sealed service evaluation are deterministic service outputs; the 7B result is the ungoverned comparison, not a claim that a language model itself became perfectly accurate.

</details>


### [16] [$\varepsilon$-MemEvo: Adaptive Cross-Task Memory Transfer for LLM Program Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12522)
*Aofan Liu,Shiyuan Song,Yiyan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ε-MemEvo is a framework for cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM-based program evolution that stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories (natural language strategy summaries) and uses adaptive injection gating to prevent negative transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based program evolution systems (like FunSearch and AlphaEvolve) optimize each task in isolation and discard search experience after completion, missing opportunities for cross-task knowledge transfer and accumulated learning.

Method: 1) Stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories - compact natural language summaries of successful algorithmic strategies rather than raw code. 2) Uses adaptive injection gate that decides whether retrieved memories should be injected and at what intensity to avoid negative transfer from semantically mismatched memories.

Result: On 8 diverse optimization benchmarks using GPT-5 backbone, ε-MemEvo improves AUCC over AdaEvolve on all 8 tasks with mean relative gain of +8.7%, improves early-stage convergence by +9.4% on average, and incurs less than 1% computational overhead. Ablations show naive memory injection can fail catastrophically while adaptive gating remains safe.

Conclusion: ε-MemEvo successfully enables cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM program evolution through task-agnostic tactic memories and adaptive injection gating, achieving significant performance improvements with minimal computational overhead while maintaining safety against negative transfer.

Abstract: LLM-based program evolution systems such as FunSearch and AlphaEvolve have shown strong ability to discover novel algorithms, but typically optimize each task in isolation, discarding search experience after completion. We introduce $\varepsilon$-MemEvo, a framework for cross-task knowledge transfer in LLM program evolution. $\varepsilon$-MemEvo stores prior experience as task-agnostic tactic memories: compact natural-language summaries of successful algorithmic strategies rather than raw code, enabling transfer across tasks with different APIs and evaluators. To avoid negative transfer from semantically mismatched memories, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo uses an adaptive injection gate that decides whether retrieved memories should be injected, and at what intensity. We evaluate $\varepsilon$-MemEvo on 8 diverse optimization benchmarks spanning mathematical optimization and systems engineering, using a content-level Leave-One-Out protocol that excludes target-task memory entries. On the primary GPT-5 backbone, $\varepsilon$-MemEvo improves AUCC over AdaEvolve on all 8 tasks, with a mean relative gain of +8.7%, and improves early-stage convergence by +9.4% on average. Ablations show that naive memory injection can fail catastrophically, while adaptive gating remains safe across all five ablation tasks. The data-updated posterior is interpretable in observed states: it favors skip during improving search and shifts from skip to hint across early and late plateaus. These gains incur less than 1% computational overhead.

</details>


### [17] [CAS: A Causal Attribution Score for Local and Global Explainable Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12555)
*Michael Georgiades,Charalambia Varnava*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a framework for causal explanation that differs from predictive explanation methods by attributing intervention effects on real-world outcomes rather than just model outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive explanation methods like SHAP only attribute model outputs but fail to attribute actual intervention effects on real-world outcomes. There's a need to separate what predicts outcomes from what explains heterogeneity in causal effects.

Method: CAS uses an identified interventional coalition game, allocates joint intervention contrast with causal Shapley contributions, and converts these effects into Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two Global CAS summaries. It's not a new Shapley formula but a local-to-global causal reporting layer with explicit intervention target.

Result: In simulations (n=2,200 each), coalition-aware CAS achieved mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107 vs 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalization and 0.213 for global normalized absolute ATE vector. Advantage increased with interaction strength. In empirical datasets (401(k) eligibility and Pennsylvania reemployment bonus), predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings differed materially from Feature-CAS rankings for treatment-effect modifiers.

Conclusion: CAS provides added value by separating outcome prediction from causal effect heterogeneity explanation. It successfully identifies different important features than predictive methods, demonstrating the need for causal rather than just predictive attribution methods.

Abstract: Predictive explanation methods attribute a model output; they do not, by themselves, attribute an intervention effect on the real-world outcome. We introduce the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a compact score architecture for causal explanation. CAS starts from an identified interventional coalition game, allocates the joint intervention contrast with causal Shapley contributions, and converts those raw outcome-scale effects into Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two complementary Global CAS summaries. The innovation is not a new Shapley formula, but a local-to-global causal reporting layer with an explicit intervention target. In the known-truth benchmark, eight repeated primary-interaction simulations (n = 2,200 each, three actions) gave mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107 for coalition-aware CAS, compared with 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalisation and 0.213 for a global normalised absolute ATE vector. The paired advantage over one-at-a-time normalisation increased from -0.003 under additivity to 0.091 under strong interactions. On both empirical DoubleML datasets, 401(k) eligibility/net financial assets (n = 9,915) and Pennsylvania reemployment bonus/unemployment duration (n = 5,099), predictive SHAP/TreeSHAP rankings differed materially from Feature-CAS rankings of treatment-effect modifiers. In Pennsylvania, dep1 (exactly one dependent) moved from predictive global rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2 and was the leading local Feature-CAS modifier. These results isolate the added value of separating what predicts the outcome from what explains heterogeneity in an estimated causal effect.

</details>


### [18] [Trie Automata for Constrained Decoding over Large Finite Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12574)
*Xingzi Xu,Karim Bouyarmane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trie automaton achieves 29X faster constrained decoding for finite string sets via Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching, maintaining 100% output validity with flat per-step cost regardless of set size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current constrained decoding systems become prohibitively slow when dealing with large finite sets of valid strings (thousands of values), creating a "cardinality wall" due to general-purpose grammar compilation overhead.

Method: Introduces a trie automaton that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) using Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks, enabling a stateless serving path that bypasses guided decoding pipelines.

Result: Achieves 7X faster per-step valid-token computation (0.65 us vs. 5.8 us) compared to XGrammar, 2-6.5X faster compilation, and 29X higher end-to-end throughput (219 req/s vs. 7.5 req/s at batch size 256), with sub-100ms compilation up to K=10,000 across seven tokenizer families.

Conclusion: The trie automaton provides an efficient solution for constrained decoding of finite string sets by combining algorithmic speedups with integration-path savings through precomputed masks, overcoming the cardinality wall while guaranteeing 100% output validity.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly need to generate structured outputs that conform to predefined schemas, with one common constraint being selection from a finite set of valid strings. Current constrained decoding systems handle this through general-purpose grammar compilation, which becomes prohibitively slow as the number of valid values grows into the thousands, a cardinality wall. We introduce the trie automaton, a specialized mechanism that exploits finite-set structure (shared prefixes, bounded depth, known cardinality) via Aho-Corasick multi-pattern matching to precompute per-node token masks. The trie achieves 7X faster per-step valid-token computation (0.65 us vs. 5.8 us) compared to XGrammar, one of the primary backends in vLLM and SGLang, and 2--6.5X faster compilation at K >= 300. Because precomputed masks enable a stateless serving path that bypasses the guided decoding pipeline, this advantage compounds in batch serving: end-to-end vLLM throughput reaches 219 req/s vs. XGrammar's 7.5 req/s at batch size 256 (29X). The 29X combines the algorithmic speedup with integration-path savings that only precomputed masks can unlock. Across seven tokenizer families (32K--262K vocabulary), the trie maintains sub-100ms compilation up to K = 10,000 and flat per-step cost regardless of set size, while guaranteeing 100% output validity.

</details>


### [19] [Reasoning Jury: Multi-Model Consensus for Evaluating Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12585)
*Congchao Wang,Diwakar Singh,Qiaozi Gao,Spyros Matsoukas,Yang Liu,Mahdi Namazifar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Reasoning Jury is a system that replaces single LLM judges with a jury of models and moderated consensus mechanism to better identify reasoning defects in long reasoning traces, outperforming frontier models at lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single LLM judges (even frontier models) perform poorly at identifying reasoning defects in long reasoning traces, which is crucial for reasoning data curation, training signals in reinforcement learning, and model evaluation. Frontier models also can't be used due to terms-of-use guardrails during online training.

Method: Replace single judge with a jury of LLMs (including open-weight models like gpt-oss-120b) using moderated consensus mechanism. A moderator conducts deliberations where jurors critique each other's judgments and modify initial votes, deriving consensus through discussion or judgment consolidation.

Result: Reasoning Jury with open-weight models significantly outperforms frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, gemini-3.1-pro) at identifying reasoning defects. The jury system costs only 8-15% of running frontier models in LLM-as-a-judge setup. Also enables deeper understanding of reasoning LLM failure modes on benchmarks.

Conclusion: The Reasoning Jury system provides a more effective and cost-efficient approach for evaluating reasoning traces, enabling better understanding of model reasoning behaviors and supporting improvements in reasoning LLMs through accurate defect identification.

Abstract: Improving reasoning LLMs requires the ability to judge the quality of long reasoning traces for effective reasoning data curation, strong training signals during reinforcement learning, and an in-depth understanding of reasoning behaviors during model performance evaluation. Additionally, surfacing reasoning mistakes that the model makes would enable improving the model's performance at runtime through providing feedback. Due to the difficulty of this complex task on long reasoning traces, single-model judges (even frontier models) do not do well at identifying reasoning defects. Additionally, leveraging frontier models during online training of reasoning LLMs is generally prohibited due to guardrails in terms of use. In this work, we introduce Reasoning Jury, a system that replaces the single judge with a jury of LLMs and a moderated consensus mechanism, to improve the fidelity of judgments for identifying reasoning defects. In reasoning jury, defects of a reasoning trace and their severity are surfaced through a deliberation where a moderator conducts a discussion amongst the jury where the jurors critique each other's judgments and get to modify their initial votes. The moderator derives a consensus through deliberation amongst jurors or consolidation of judgements. We show that Reasoning Jury with a jury of open-weight models (e.g., gpt-oss-120b) is able to significantly outperform frontier models (opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, and gemini-3.1-pro) at correctly identifying reasoning defects. Besides accuracy performance improvements, the aggregated cost of the jury (initial verdicts, deliberations, consolidation, etc.) is a fraction (8 to 15%) of the cost of running frontier models in LLM-as-a-judge setup. We also show how these judgements can be leveraged to understand failure modes of reasoning LLMs on benchmarks, which allows much deeper understanding of a model's performance.

</details>


### [20] [Auditable agentic AI for evidence-grounded thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12590)
*Haifan Gong,Shiyu Chen,Bodong Wang,Yuqi Wang,Shijie Wang,Guoliang You,Xinyu Xiong,Haowei Wang,Mingzhi Mao,Dexing Kong,Qinghua Liu,Wei Lou,Fei Chen,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ThyroidXAgent is an interactive AI system for comprehensive thyroid ultrasound diagnosis that coordinates lesion segmentation, classification, and report generation with clinician oversight, showing improved accuracy and efficiency across multiple datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI systems for thyroid ultrasound diagnosis typically address individual tasks in isolation and provide limited clinical review support. There's a need for coordinated diagnostic tools with auditable evidence records and clinician interaction.

Method: Developed ThyroidXAgent using OpenThyroidDB (multicentre resource with ~0.3M images and 24K reports), creating an agentic AI system that coordinates specialized diagnostic tools and stores outputs as auditable case-level evidence records.

Result: Achieved 87.21% Dice score for nodule segmentation and 0.9466 AUROC for benign-malignant classification. Improved physician classification accuracy, increased report consistency from 70.3% to 86.2%, and reduced segmentation and reporting time by 35.9% and 27.4% respectively.

Conclusion: The study supports auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI systems for thyroid ultrasound diagnosis, showing they can improve diagnostic consistency, accuracy, and efficiency while maintaining clinician oversight.

Abstract: Thyroid ultrasound diagnosis requires coordinated lesion localization, measurement, risk stratification and reporting, yet most AI systems address these tasks in isolation and provide limited support for clinical review. We present ThyroidXAgent, a clinician-interactive agentic AI system that coordinates specialized diagnostic tools and stores their outputs as an auditable case-level evidence record. The system was developed using OpenThyroidDB, a multicentre, multitask resource integrating approximately 0.3 million ultrasound images and 24,000 paired reports, and was evaluated on 28,458 non-overlapping test cases, including 8,721 cases from 35 centres in the private NHC-MISD-TUS cohort. Across heterogeneous datasets, ThyroidXAgent achieved a mean Dice score of 87.21 percent for nodule segmentation and a mean AUROC of 0.9466 for benign-malignant classification. The same workflow supported lymph-node metastasis prediction and follicular versus papillary thyroid carcinoma classification, with AUROCs of 0.864 and 0.805, respectively. For report generation, evidence-grounded assembly outperformed multimodal language-model baselines across three cohorts. ThyClinScore, a lesion-level clinical semantic metric introduced here, showed the strongest correlation with a location-aware language-model judge. ThyroidXAgent improved physician classification accuracy, increased report diagnostic consistency from 70.3 percent to 86.2 percent, and reduced segmentation and reporting time by 35.9 percent and 27.4 percent, respectively. These findings support auditable, clinician-correctable agentic AI for thyroid ultrasound diagnosis and reporting.

</details>


### [21] [DiG-bench: Discovery in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12593)
*Ruairidh M. Battleday,Kai Sandbrink,Jimi Cullen-Drohan,Zihan Yan,Timothy Muller,Clare Maguire,Ales Kubicek,Fraser Greenlee-Scott,Sukrit Sumant,Tri Dao,Jürgen Schmidhuber,Michal Valko,Joshua Tenenbaum,Thomas L. Griffiths,Zeb Kurth-Nelson,James C. R. Whittington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiG-bench is a new benchmark designed to test AI agents' ability to discover novel knowledge through experimentation in controlled environments, featuring 70 independent games with unique transformation rules that must be discovered through interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a gap in current AI benchmarks for evaluating systems' capacity to discover new knowledge through experimentation, particularly in controlled environments where objectives are unknown. Most existing benchmarks focus on known objectives rather than true discovery.

Method: Created DiG-bench with 70 independent games, each encoded as short strings with unique transformation rules. Games have seven difficulty tiers and present challenges to test rule discovery. Win conditions are also unknown and must be discovered.

Result: All 70 games were solvable by at least one human on first attempt. Lowest difficulty tier is routinely solvable by multiple AI models, while highest tier challenges best models. Public release includes 21 games with remainder held private for secure evaluation.

Conclusion: DiG-bench addresses a critical gap in AI evaluation by providing a benchmark for testing discovery capabilities. It enables assessment of how well AI systems can formulate novel generalizations through experimentation in controlled environments.

Abstract: Discovery---formulating novel generalizations---is a central part of the scientific process. Despite its importance, there is a gap in the current AI benchmark landscape, with few benchmarks directly probing the capacity for discovering new knowledge with experimentation in controlled environments where the objective is unknown. To address this gap, we release a new benchmark: DiG-bench (Discovery in Games). DiG-bench consists of a set of 70 independent games. Each game is encoded as a short string and has unique transformation rules that must be discovered through interaction and experimentation. The levels of the game present a series of challenges to test whether the rules have been discovered, where the win conditions for each level are also unknown. We provide games at seven tiers of difficulty for AI agents. The lowest tier is routinely solvable by multiple models, while the highest tier challenges the best models in agentic harnesses. All 70 games were solved by at least one human on first attempt. A subset of 21 games is released publicly, and the remainder is held private for secure evaluation.

</details>


### [22] [Dead text or binding clause? Measuring and restoring constraint influence in black-box LLM dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12599)
*Haoyuan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Sysname addresses the problem of behavioral relapse in multi-turn dialogues where models fail to properly revoke withdrawn constraints, introducing a system to measure, predict, and repair this failure using model APIs alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current dialogue systems have a critical flaw: when users revoke constraints in multi-turn conversations, models often continue to enact withdrawn requirements (sometimes even asserting their removal). This "behavioral relapse" or "revocation inertia" is currently unmeasured, unpredictable, and unrepairable with existing tools.

Method: The Sysname system uses three components: 1) A contract ledger that pairs constraints with executable checkers, records revocations as tombstones, and compiles net constraint states into single specifications; 2) A sequential ablation probe that measures per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effects; 3) A repair ladder that operates under token- and attempt-matched budgets.

Result: Experiments show relapse increases with constraint load (from ~2% to ~8% at 8B scale), while stronger models have minimal relapse. Ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse compared to baseline (statistically significant). The probe predicts relapse before delivery (AUROC >0.5), and tombstone notes recover about one-third of the compilation effect. Adaptive ladder interventions provide no additional detectable gain.

Conclusion: Revocation failure can be transformed from an invisible property into a measurable, predictable, and repairable aspect of dialogue state management, achieved with modest API compute overhead (1.4x delivery cost and ~1% API compute per result).

Abstract: Multi-turn dialogues let users revoke constraints as easily as impose them, but revocation does not reliably take effect: models keep enacting withdrawn requirements (occasionally beneath comments asserting their removal), a failure we call \emph{behavioral relapse}, or revocation inertia. No existing instrument measures this influence per clause, predicts it before delivery, or repairs it under matched budgets. \sysname{} closes the three gaps through the model API alone: a contract ledger pairs every constraint with an executable checker, records revocations as tombstones, and compiles the net constraint state ahead of time into a single specification; a sequential ablation probe measures per-clause adherence and incremental behavioral effect; a repair ladder operates under token- and attempt-matched budgets. On \dataname{} (\NTasks{} HumanEval tasks, \NClauses{} verified checkers), relapse at an 8B operating point climbs from \ScaleDelayedMTwo{} to \ScaleDelayedMEight{} as constraint load grows, while stronger models sit at floor. Under matched checkers, model, and budget, ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse against a no-ledger verifier-retry baseline (\RestoreDiff{}, 95\% CI \RestoreDiffCI{}, $p$ \RestoreDiffP{}); adaptive ladder interventions stacked on top add no detectable gain (95\% confidence excludes gains $\geq$ \LadderExcludedGain{}). The probe predicts relapse before delivery (AUROC \AurocPrimary{}); a one-sentence tombstone note recovers about a third of the compilation effect and survives a placebo control. At \CostDeliveryFactor{} delivery overhead and \CostTotalHedged{} of API compute for every result, revocation failure becomes a measurable, predictable, and repairable property of dialogue state rather than an invisible one.

</details>


### [23] [@skills: Attention is all you have](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12610)
*Li Yin,Zhi Li,Zhan Shi,Haoran Zhang,Haebin Seong,Zhangyang,Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: @skills protocol separates skill content from prompt residency, enabling skill usage without installation by using Git-tracked paths and .gitignore-style triggers, paired with an optional hub for discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current skill delivery model requires installation, competing for limited prompt slots (less than 100), leaving long-tail skills unusable and forcing teams' playbooks to compete for scarce space.

Method: Proposes @skills protocol that separates installation into three functions: content, persistence, and automatic triggering. Uses paths to address skills, Git-tracked trees for adaptation, and .gitignore-style lines for prompt residency. No manifest, lockfile, or registration required.

Result: Open protocol implemented in AdaL CLI, paired with free hub (atskills.one) for search, ranking, hosting, and authoring. Any agent that can read files/run commands becomes a client via single instruction file. GitHub skills retain original identities.

Conclusion: @skills enables "install less, use more" approach by decoupling skill usage from prompt residency, making skills accessible without installation while maintaining compatibility with existing workflows.

Abstract: There are 56,804 public agent skills today, and teams write many more privately. The dominant delivery model is installation: once installed, a skill's description remains in the system prompt, competing for fewer than 100 reliable trigger slots. This leaves the long tail with no practical path to use and forces teams' own playbooks to compete for the same scarce space. We observe that installation bundles three separable functions: content, persistence, and automatic triggering. Only the last requires prompt residency. We therefore propose @skills, an open protocol that separates them. A path addresses any skill, subtree, or collection, and reading a skill is sufficient to use it, so nothing is installed or made resident. The operation vendors a copy at the same path into a project's Git-tracked tree for adaptation and ownership. The operation adds one .gitignore-style line, the only element that costs prompt residency. A directory is a menu, making bundles ordinary directories rather than all-or-nothing units. The protocol requires no manifest, lockfile, or registration, and SKILL.md remains unchanged. @skills is additive, ships as an installable package, and turns any agent that can read files and run commands into a client through a single instruction file. Its open specification is at https://github.com/SylphAI-Inc/atskills and it is implemented in the AdaL CLI at https://adalagent.ai . Because paths address skills well but cannot find them, the protocol is paired with a free hub at https://atskills.one for corpus-wide search and ranking, repository-free hosting, private and team collections, and one-screen authoring. The hub is optional: gh: and local paths resolve without it, and indexed GitHub skills retain their gh: identities. Install less, use more.

</details>


### [24] [Jagged Judges: Epistemic Stability Under Silence, Pressure, and Persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12645)
*Justin Zhao,Himaghna Bhattacharjee,Hannah Korevaar,Bhaktipriya Radharapu,Khalid El-Arini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Wiggle Framework provides a unified stress test for evaluating LLM judges' epistemic stability across mechanical consistency, single-turn conviction, and multi-turn persistence, revealing substantial verdict flipping under pushback with mostly corrupting effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM judge evaluations rely heavily on accuracy metrics, which don't assess how stable judges are under re-prompting, challenges, or sustained pushback, creating a gap in understanding their true robustness.

Method: The Wiggle Framework decomposes judge robustness into three dimensions: Mechanical Consistency (stability under re-prompting/reframing), Single-turn Conviction (stability under single challenge), and Multi-turn Persistence (stability under sustained/adaptive pressure). Applied to 9 frontier models across 14 tasks including safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, and political-response evaluation.

Result: Every model exhibited substantial wiggle: 25-71% verdict flipping under static pushback, and 62-91% with adversarial LLM persuader. Pressure that succeeds in changing verdicts is almost always net-corrupting relative to ground truth. Baseline jury majority strength emerged as the most effective single-shot signal for anticipating which items will wiggle.

Conclusion: LLM judges are surprisingly unstable under pressure, with substantial verdict flipping rates. The Wiggle Framework provides the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of epistemic stability, revealing critical vulnerabilities in current judging systems that accuracy metrics alone cannot detect.

Abstract: LLM judges have become central infrastructure for model evaluations, online grading, and reward modeling. Judges are typically validated by accuracy on golden data, but accuracy says little about whether they are stable under re-prompting, challenge, or sustained pushback. We introduce the \emph{Wiggle Framework}, a unified stress test for epistemic stability in LLM judges. The framework decomposes judge robustness along three dimensions: Mechanical Consistency (stability under re-prompting and reframing), Single-turn Conviction (stability under a single challenge), and Multi-turn Persistence (stability under sustained or adaptive pressure). We use the framework to study 9 frontier models across 14 judging tasks spanning safety, toxicity, AI writing detection, and political-response evaluation. Every model exhibits substantial wiggle as a judge --- flipping verdicts 25--71\% of the time under static pushback, and 62--91\% with an adversarial LLM persuader. Critically, we find that pressure that succeeds in changing a judge's verdict is almost always net-corrupting with respect to ground truth. Beyond the framework itself, we identify baseline jury majority strength as the most effective single-shot signal for anticipating which items wiggle. Taken together, this is the first apples-to-apples cross-dataset comparison of mechanical, conformity, and persuadability tests in a judging context.

</details>


### [25] [SteerBench-Work: A Benchmark for Agent Steering at Action Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12654)
*Oguz Serdar,Cuneyt Mertayak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SteerBench-Work is a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents' steering decisions (proceed vs. hold) in workplace scenarios, showing models consistently over-refuse authorized actions while rarely allowing unsafe ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents perform critical workplace actions (emails, payments, etc.) but need reliable steering mechanisms to decide whether to proceed or hold actions for human review. Current models lack calibrated decision-making at this critical commit boundary.

Method: Created SteerBench-Work with 106 incident-anchored scenarios across multiple domains, including evidence-reversed mirrors and calibration controls. Models see proposed actions and evidence, make gate decisions, and are scored on accuracy.

Result: Models show severe directional bias: wrongly hold authorized work 28.1% of opportunities but wrongly allow unsafe work only 1.0%. Performance drops on evidence-reversed mirrors (63.8% vs 98.5% on original incidents), showing poor risk calibration.

Conclusion: General capability doesn't equal steering calibration; higher-capability models often over-refuse. Need specialized calibration for workplace agent steering decisions beyond basic reasoning abilities.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents act through tools, and a single step can send an email, merge a pull request, or wire a payment. The steering decision is the pre-commit choice at that boundary: proceed, or hold for human or policy review.
