We introduce SteerBench-Work, an incident-anchored, bidirectional benchmark for that decision in workplace agents across developer operations, customer service, finance, legal, medical, HR, and security.
  Release v2026-05 contains 106 scenarios anchored in public incidents, paired evidence-reversed mirrors, and calibration controls, with labels split nearly evenly between proceed and hold so the two error directions get near-identical numbers of chances. A model sees the proposed action and the available evidence, returns a gate decision, and is scored on whether it crosses or holds the boundary correctly. Across 30 model conditions the failures run almost entirely in one direction: models wrongly hold authorized, evidence-cleared work on 28.1% of opportunities and wrongly allow unsafe work on 1.0%. The hardest cases are risk-resolved commits, where signed or structured evidence has already cleared a real risk trigger, and models score markedly worse on evidence-reversed mirrors of famous incidents (63.8%) than on the incidents themselves (98.5%). General capability is not the same as steering calibration: higher-capability models often over-refuse at the commit boundary, and more reasoning can repair a weak gate while leaving a calibrated one flat. The public leaderboard is at steerbench.com.

</details>


### [26] [General Probabilities of Causation with Causal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12657)
*Xin Shu,Zhen Lei,Ang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tighter bounds for multivalued probabilities of causation by incorporating causal information from covariates and mediators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior research has derived bounds for probabilities of causation (PoCs) in binary settings and extended them to multivalued settings, but there's an open question whether additional causal knowledge (like covariates and mediators) can further tighten these bounds in multivalued scenarios.

Method: The authors derive tighter theoretical bounds for multivalued PoCs by incorporating causal information encoded in covariates and mediators, building on previous work by Tian and Pearl, Mueller et al., Li and Pearl, and Shu et al.

Result: The paper shows that incorporating causal information from covariates and mediators does indeed tighten the bounds for multivalued PoCs. The theoretical results are illustrated with toy examples, and simulation studies demonstrate that the proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

Conclusion: Additional causal knowledge (covariates and mediators) can be effectively leveraged to obtain tighter bounds for multivalued probabilities of causation, advancing the partial identification framework for individual causal effects.

Abstract: Probabilities of causation (PoCs) characterize individual causal responses that cannot be directly observed and therefore generally require partial identification. Tian and Pearl first derived theoretically sharp bounds for binary PoCs, including the probability of necessity (PN), the probability of sufficiency (PS), and the probability of necessity and sufficiency (PNS). Mueller et al. subsequently tightened the bounds for binary PNS by incorporating causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. More recently, Li and Pearl, as well as Shu et al., extended PoCs to multivalued settings and derived corresponding theoretical bounds. These developments naturally raise the question of whether additional causal knowledge can further tighten the bounds in multivalued settings. This paper addresses this question by deriving tighter bounds for multivalued PoCs through the incorporation of causal information encoded in covariates and mediators. We illustrate the theoretical results with toy examples, while simulation studies further demonstrate that the proposed bounds are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

</details>


### [27] [Designing AI Pipelines for Decision-Ready ITSM Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12670)
*Archan Dutta,Yash Dharmadhikari,Marat Valiullin,Rahul Guha,Alexander Liss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI pipeline transforms raw ITSM ticket data into multi-level decision-support artifacts using LLM-based normalization and clustering techniques, achieving high ratings for interpretability, actionability, trust, and usability from stakeholders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ITSM systems accumulate large volumes of heterogeneous ticket data that are difficult for sales and executive stakeholders to convert into actionable intelligence, creating a need for better analytics solutions.

Method: A sociotechnical AI pipeline designed following design science research principles that combines LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical agglomerative clustering to generate executive-facing Main-topics and granular Sub-topics.

Result: Stakeholder evaluation across six artifacts and five raters shows all four decision-support metrics (interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use) exceed 4.0/5.0 on average, with trust being the most consistent signal.

Conclusion: ITSM analytics should be positioned as an Information Systems problem of transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design, with the developed pipeline effectively bridging the gap between raw data and executive decision-making.

Abstract: IT service management (ITSM) systems accumulate large volumes of heterogeneous ticket data that are difficult for sales and executive stakeholders to convert into actionable intelligence. This paper presents a sociotechnical AI pipeline, designed and evaluated following design science research principles, that transforms raw ITSM exports into a multilevel decision-support artifact. The pipeline combines LLM-based schema normalization, HDBSCAN sub-topic clustering, and hierarchical agglomerative clustering to generate executive-facing Main-topics and granular Sub-topics. A stakeholder evaluation across six artifacts and five raters from Sales Engineering and customer success roles shows that all four decision-support metrics, interpretability, actionability, trust, and likelihood of use, on average exceed 4.0 out of 5.0, with trust as the most consistent signal. The findings position ITSM analytics as an Information Systems (IS) problem of transformation, abstraction, and human-centered design.

</details>


### [28] [On the Expressive Power of Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12671)
*Phokion Kolaitis,Rik Sengupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper surveys results that analyze the expressive power of transformers (core components of LLMs) using circuit complexity theory, comparing their computational capabilities against established circuit classes based on various resource constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers form the fundamental building blocks of modern large language models, and there's growing interest in precisely understanding their computational capabilities by comparing them against established theoretical models from computer science.

Method: The paper uses circuit complexity theory as the primary analytical framework, examining how transformers parameterized by different resources (attention mechanisms, precision) correspond to various circuit classes defined by resources like gate types, size, and depth.

Result: The analysis establishes circuit complexity as the appropriate theoretical framework for understanding transformer expressive power, showing how different transformer configurations map to specific circuit classes, thereby delineating their computational capabilities.

Conclusion: Circuit complexity provides a "correct" and systematic approach to analyze transformer expressive power, allowing precise comparisons between transformer architectures (parameterized by attention and precision) and traditional circuit classes, offering insights into the fundamental computational capabilities of modern LLMs.

Abstract: Multi-layer transformers form the critical component of essentially all large language models (LLMs) in use today. Because of their ubiquity and computational capability, there is a rapidly growing body of work that aims to precisely calibrate the expressive power of transformers as language recognizers by comparing them against standard models of computation studied for decades by the theoretical computer science community. In this endeavor, circuit complexity has by and large emerged as the "correct" branch of computational complexity to analyze the expressive power of transformers; the reason is that parameterizing transformers by the various resources they use, such as attention and precision, leads to direct comparisons with different classes of circuits parameterized by resources such as type of gates, size, and depth. Here, we present an overview of selected results that delineate the expressive power of transformers using concepts and methods from circuit complexity.

</details>


### [29] [Lines and Ladders: A Context-Aware Multi-Agent Framework for Large-Scale Retail Price Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12674)
*Ravi Teja Chunduri,Srikaran Reddy Boya,Deep Narayan Mishra,Ajay Kumar B,Karthik Kumaran,Pranay Kona*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent LLM framework automates price taxonomy construction for global retailers to maintain consistent pricing across millions of items, achieving high accuracy in production deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global retailers need to maintain price consistency and Every Day Low Price strategies across millions of items, but manual governance of price relationships is impossible. Inconsistent pricing across item variants distorts customer value perception and causes sales cannibalization.

Method: A scalable, context-aware Multi-Agent Framework using specialized LLM agents to construct "Lines and Ladders" pricing taxonomies. The framework identifies key attributes, extracts multi-modal values, and applies hierarchical grouping logic with a 3-Agent system designed to mitigate cognitive overload found in single-agent approaches.

Result: The system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines. It achieves >90% precision and >75% recall in Food & Consumables, and 80.2% assignment accuracy in unstructured General Merchandise catalog. The framework has been evaluated on real-world enterprise data and deployed in production.

Conclusion: The multi-agent LLM framework successfully automates price taxonomy construction at scale, enabling global retailers to maintain price consistency across their catalogs. The approach demonstrates significant improvements over single-agent methods by distributing cognitive tasks across specialized agents.

Abstract: Maintaining price consistency and executing an Every Day Low Price strategy is critical for global retailers. However, with catalogs spanning millions of active items, manual governance of price relationships is infeasible. Inconsistent pricing across item variants distorts customer value perception and cannibalizes sales. To address this, we present a scalable, context-aware Multi-Agent Framework designed to automate the construction of "Lines and Ladders" pricing taxonomies. Our framework employs specialized LLM agents to construct these coherent pricing structures by identifying key attributes, extracting multi-modal values, and applying hierarchical grouping logic. Evaluated on real-world enterprise data and deployed in production, our 3-Agent system achieves an F1-score of 0.83 for Lines, outperforming single-agent baselines by mitigating cognitive overload. The system achieves >90% precision and >75% recall in Food & Consumables, and 80.2% assignment accuracy in the unstructured General Merchandise catalog.

</details>


### [30] [Privacy-Preserving RAG by Concealing Sensitive Information from External LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12675)
*Saleh Almohaimeed,Saad Almohaimeed,Mousa Jari,Fahad Alotaibi,Khalid A. Alobaid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SEAG is a privacy-preserving framework for RAG systems that replaces sensitive entities with aliases before sending queries/documents to external generators, protecting confidentiality while maintaining answer quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG privacy research focuses on preventing unauthorized access, but overlooks that external generators have access to queries and retrieved documents containing sensitive information, creating potential misuse risks.

Method: SEAG uses a lightweight model to: 1) locate sensitive entities, 2) generate corresponding aliases, 3) construct an entity replacement table, which is used to replace sensitive words in queries and retrieved documents before sending to external generators.

Result: All SEAG models achieved over 80% accuracy on the User metric (providing correct responses while hiding sensitive info). For hiding all sensitive entities, Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 achieved 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91% total accuracies respectively.

Conclusion: SEAG successfully enables users to leverage powerful third-party generators in RAG systems without disclosing sensitive information, addressing an overlooked privacy concern in existing RAG research.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is widely used to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in answering user queries. Existing privacy research on RAG has focused on preventing unauthorized users from accessing sensitive data. However, another important problem that is often overlooked in RAG privacy research is that external generators have access to the query and the retrieved documents, which may contain confidential information that could potentially be misused or accessed for unintended purposes. In this paper, we introduce the Sensitive Entity Alias Generator (SEAG), a privacy-preserving framework that empowers users to utilize powerful third-party generators without disclosing sensitive information. SEAG introduces a lightweight model that locates sensitive entities, generates corresponding aliases, and constructs an entity replacement table. The table is used to replace sensitive words in the user's query and in the retrieved documents before they are forwarded to an external generator. For this purpose, two datasets were constructed: one for fine-tuning SEAG models to generate entity replacement tables, and another for evaluating the entire SEAG framework. The experimental results demonstrate the success of the SEAG framework. As for the User metric, which measures the ability of the model to provide a correct response to the user while hiding sensitive information from the external generator, all SEAG models achieved over 80% accuracy. Additional analysis further evaluated the ability of SEAG models Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 to hide all sensitive entities within given documents. The results show good performance with total accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91%, respectively.

</details>


### [31] [The Role of Natural Language Understanding in Multimodal Video-Based Dengue Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12677)
*Danial Sharifrazi,Saadat Behzadi,Julakha Jahan Jui,Mojtaba Mohammadi,Nouman Javed,Roohallah Alizadehsani,Prasad N. Paradkar,Asim Bhatti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A YOLO-CLIP vision-language framework achieves high accuracy in classifying flight frames of infected vs. uninfected dengue mosquitoes from video data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Detecting infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes is challenging due to their small size, rapid/irregular movement, and environmental factors like background, lighting, and shadows that make reliable feature extraction difficult.

Method: Proposes a YOLO-CLIP vision-language framework: 1) YOLO isolates mosquito regions from background, 2) visual features from frames are aligned with biologically meaningful textual prompts in shared embedding space, 3) multimodal model fine-tuned with supervised bidirectional contrastive learning, and 4) evaluated through frame-level image-text similarity-based classification.

Result: Achieved 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity at frame level. After temporal aggregation, achieved complete video-level performance. Ablation showed fine-tuning and CLIP-based representations were essential, while textual branch provided semantic alignment rather than accuracy advantage over vision-only model.

Conclusion: Vision-language models provide a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data, particularly for challenging biological observation tasks where environmental factors complicate feature extraction.

Abstract: Detecting infection-related behavioral changes in mosquitoes from video data is challenging because mosquitoes are small, move rapidly and irregularly, and are affected by environmental factors such as background, lighting, and shadows, which can make reliable feature extraction difficult. In this study, a YOLO- and Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP)-based vision-language framework is proposed to classify mosquito flight frames of uninfected and Dengue virus serotype 2 (DENV2)-infected mosquitoes. First, YOLO is used to isolate mosquito regions from the background. Then, visual features extracted from video frames are aligned with biologically meaningful textual prompts in a shared embedding space. The multimodal model was fine-tuned using supervised bidirectional contrastive learning and evaluated through frame-level image-text similarity-based classification. The results show that the proposed method achieved 98.54% accuracy and 99.91% sensitivity at the frame level. After temporal aggregation of frame-level information, the model achieved complete video-level performance. The ablation results showed that fine-tuning and CLIP-based representations were essential for this domain, while the textual branch provided semantic image-text alignment rather than an accuracy advantage over the vision-only model. These findings suggest that vision-language models can provide a useful framework for analyzing infection-related biological behaviors from video data.

</details>


### [32] [Beyond the Best Guess: Improving LLM Solution Coverage with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12679)
*Conor F. Hayes,Elliot Meyerson,Kajetan Schweighofer,Roberto Dailey,Babak Hodjat,Risto Miikkulainen,Xin Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ES post-training for LLMs outperforms RL in discovery tasks by maintaining diverse solution coverage, achieving better pass@k results and improved performance on math benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In discovery domains like math and science, LLMs need to explore solution spaces beyond single best guesses, but standard RL post-training narrows output distributions and reduces solution coverage, limiting pass@k performance.

Method: Uses Evolution Strategies (ES) - a population-based, gradient-free post-training method that optimizes directly in weight space through random perturbations, maintaining broader output distributions compared to RL.

Result: ES achieves consistently higher pass@k than RL, produces broader output distributions with greater solution coverage, and enables better performance on standard math benchmarks.

Conclusion: ES provides a superior foundation for LLM post-training in discovery problems and other domains where diverse solution coverage is critical, addressing limitations of RL's output distribution narrowing.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in discovery domains such as math and science. The usual approach is to present the problem to the model and use its answer as the proposed solution. However, beyond this best guess, discovery can be enhanced by increasing test-time compute. In a process called pass@k, the model is allowed to explore the solution space and generate diverse candidate solutions. Unfortunately, the standard approach to post-training LLMs through Reinforcement Learning (RL) may limit pass@k: the model's output distribution narrows around high-reward outputs, causing the solution coverage to collapse. The alternative is to use Evolution Strategies (ES), a population-based, gradient-free post-training method that optimizes directly in weight space through random perturbations. As this paper shows, ES achieves consistently higher pass@k than RL and produces a broader output distribution with greater solution coverage. This coverage in turn makes it possible to achieve better results in e.g. standard math benchmarks. Thus, ES provides a better foundation for post-training in discovery problems and other domains where diverse solution coverage is critical.

</details>


### [33] [Spatial Memory Agent: Experience-Grounded Procedure Memory for Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12743)
*Haokai Zhang,Yuhang Ding,Yunshu Zhou,Xinze Du,Shengtao Zhang,Zhiyue Zhao,Yuling Xi,Hao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Spatial Memory Agent (SMA) enables frozen vision-language models to improve spatial reasoning through parameter-free self-evolution by converting verified spatial experiences into reusable lessons with transfer reliability scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for improving spatial reasoning in VLMs either require post-training (fine-tuning/RL) or depend on external spatial tools at inference time. The paper explores a complementary route: enabling frozen VLMs to self-evolve without parameter updates or external tools.

Method: SMA is an experience-grounded runtime framework that queries a frozen VLM in verifiable spatial environments, obtains predictions and rewards, uses verifier-guided reflection to distill compact transferable lessons, and assigns each lesson a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS). During deployment, it retrieves lessons via semantic filtering and similarity-TRS ranking to guide inference.

Result: Across five spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and best accuracy among evaluated methods in most of 20 evaluations, establishing a practical parameter-update-free path for spatial self-evolution.

Conclusion: SMA demonstrates that frozen VLMs can effectively improve spatial reasoning through parameter-free self-evolution by learning from verified experiences and reusing transferable lessons, offering a complementary approach to existing post-training and tool-based methods.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence is becoming a foundation for embodied agents, robotic planning, and multimodal assistants. To improve the spatial reasoning ability of VLM agents, existing work has mainly followed two lines. One line uses post-training methods, such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Another line adopts an agentic paradigm in which the model calls external spatial tools, such as depth estimation and 3D reconstruction tools, to gather intermediate spatial evidence. We study a complementary and underexplored route: Can a frozen VLM agent improve its spatial reasoning through \textbf{parameter-update-free self-evolution}, without depending on external expert spatial tools at inference time? We present \textbf{Spatial Memory Agent (SMA)}, an \textbf{experience-grounded runtime framework} that converts verified spatial experience into reusable transferable lessons. In a verifiable spatial environment, SMA queries the frozen VLM, obtains a predicted answer and reward, and uses \textbf{verifier-guided reflection} to distill compact transferable lessons from spatial experience. SMA further assigns each lesson a \textbf{Transfer Reliability Score (TRS)}, which is initialized uniformly and calibrated from later retrieval outcomes as visit evidence of future transfer reliability. During \textbf{read-only deployment}, SMA retrieves lessons by semantic filter and similarity-TRS combined ranking, allowing the retrieved memory to guide frozen model inference. Across five representative spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy among the evaluated methods in most of the 20 evaluations, establishing a practical parameter-update-free path for spatial self-evolution across the evaluated frozen model scales and environments.

</details>


### [34] [Correct Is Not Governed: Provenance Integrity in Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12761)
*Jesus Salas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Matrix is a governed execution layer that provides institutional integrity for agentic workflows by recording authority/fact dependencies, verifying completion evidence, and invalidating stale work, enabling auditability and independent verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic workflow evaluation focuses only on correct outcomes, which is insufficient for institutional settings where correctness alone doesn't ensure proper authority, supported completion claims, or handling of stale work due to later changes.

Method: The authors present Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work to ensure governed execution with inspectable provenance.

Result: In controlled comparisons, governed workflows preserved governing evidence, refused unsupported closure, and limited recovery to dependent tasks. However, a role-separated transfer challenge showed that completeness contracts can over-block synthetic packets produced outside authoring context.

Conclusion: Matrix serves as an institutional integrity layer that makes agentic work auditable and independently verifiable, though it's not established as a general accuracy enhancer. The approach addresses the need for governed execution in institutional settings.

Abstract: Agentic workflows are commonly evaluated by whether they reach the correct outcome. That is insufficient in institutional settings, where a correct action may rely on the wrong authority, an unsupported completion claim, or work made stale by a later change. We define governed execution as work whose decisions, completion, and response to change are supported by inspectable provenance. We present Matrix, a deterministic causal-state layer that records authority and fact dependencies, verifies completion evidence, and selectively invalidates affected work. Across controlled comparisons, governed and direct workflows often reached the same outcomes, but only the governed path consistently preserved governing evidence, refused unsupported closure, and limited recovery to dependent tasks. A role-separated transfer challenge then failed: a deterministically enforced completeness contract severely over-blocked synthetic packets produced outside its authoring context. These results do not establish Matrix as a general accuracy enhancer; they support its primary role as an institutional integrity layer for making agentic work auditable and independently verifiable.

</details>


### [35] [PROVE-RT: Generating Mechanized Theorem Prover Scripts for Real-Time Systems using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12762)
*Sadat Shahriyar,Shareef Ahmed,Abdullah Al Arafat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PROVE-RT: An LLM-assisted framework that generates PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanized schedulability analysis verification, achieving 44.7% success rate through dependency-aware sketches and staged generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current schedulability analysis verification relies on manual pen-and-paper proofs that are difficult to scale, validate, and maintain. While mechanized verification in PROSA/ROCQ offers rigor, manually constructing proofs requires substantial domain expertise. LLMs show promise but lack PROSA-specific knowledge needed for correct verification scripts.

Method: PROVE-RT framework includes: 1) dependency-aware informal sketches, 2) retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, 3) staged skeleton generation, and 4) proof completion. The system uses a corpus of 13,134 informal sketches from 1,191 real-time systems papers.

Result: PROVE-RT achieves 44.7% success rate in generating valid PROSA mechanizations on a curated evaluation set, compared to direct LLM prompting which fails reliably. The framework demonstrates that retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automated mechanization.

Conclusion: Retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can effectively improve automated mechanization of schedulability analysis in PROSA/ROCQ, addressing the knowledge gap in current LLMs for PROSA-specific verification tasks while providing a scalable alternative to manual proof construction.

Abstract: Schedulability analysis is essential for certifying real-time systems, but existing tests are often developed through pen-and-paper proofs that are difficult to scale, validate, and maintain. Mechanized verification in PROSA/ROCQ offers a rigorous alternative, yet manually constructing such proofs requires substantial domain expertise and proof-engineering effort. Recent successes of large language models (LLMs) across a wide range of tasks make them promising candidates for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanized theorem provers. However, state-of-the-art LLMs often lack the PROSA-specific knowledge required to correctly use its modeling abstractions and proof patterns.
  This paper introduces PROVE-RT, an LLM-assisted framework for generating PROSA/ROCQ scripts to mechanize schedulability analyses in real-time systems literature. PROVE-RT guides generation through dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. We construct a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1, 191 real-time systems papers, containing 13, 134 informal sketches with dependency information. On a curated evaluation set, direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably generate valid PROSA mechanizations, whereas PROVE-RT achieves a success rate of 44.7%. These results show that retrieval-guided and staged LLM assistance can improve automated mechanization of schedulability analysis in PROSA/ROCQ.

</details>


### [36] [ARAC: Benchmarking Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness on End-to-End Researchs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12788)
*Jiale Cui,Yueyao Yuan,Kaixi Zhong,Xiaogang Xu,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARAC-Bench is a framework for evaluating Auto-Research systems by assessing how well they mimic human research processes rather than just matching final answers, revealing current systems achieve only 67.9% alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid advancement of Auto-Research has created an evaluation challenge: how to measure alignment, logical coherence, and evolutionary completeness of research trajectories compared to human research behavior. Current evaluation focuses too much on final answers rather than research processes.

Method: ARAC-Bench uses two synergistic components: 1) Academic Cognition Skills system that transforms implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated quantifiable rubrics, and 2) a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol that decomposes research into three traceable dimensions (Proposal, Experiment, Synthesis) under strict modular constraints.

Result: Evaluation of 11 state-of-the-art frameworks yields a best alignment score of only 67.9 out of 100, showing significant gaps in simulating rigorous human methodology. Validation against Ph.D. candidate rankings shows strong correlation (0.8141), confirming ARAC-Bench reliably reflects dimensions researchers value.

Conclusion: ARAC-Bench provides both a fine-grained diagnostic tool and a scalable reward signal for training next-generation autonomous research systems, addressing a fundamental evaluation gap in Auto-Research.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Auto-Research has surfaced a fundamental evaluation challenge: how can we measure the alignment, logical coherence, and evolutionary completeness of its research trajectory with human research behavior? We propose Auto-Research's Alignment and Completeness, ARAC-Bench: a Researcher-Mimicking Evaluation framework that shifts the objective from matching final answers to reproducing high-quality human research processes. The framework operates through two synergistic components: the Academic Cognition Skills system, which is the first to transforms implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated, quantifiable rubrics; and a three-stage capability diagnostic protocol, which decomposes the research process under strict modular constraints into three traceable, mutually independent dimensions: Proposal, Experiment, and Synthesis. Systematic evaluation of 11 SOTA frameworks yields a best alignment score of only 67.9 of 100, revealing a significant gap in simulating rigorous human methodology. Validation against Ph.D. Candidates rankings shows a strong correlation of 0.8141, confirming that ARAC-Bench reliably reflects the dimensions researchers truly value. ARAC-Bench provides not only a fine-grained diagnostic tool but also a scalable reward signal for training the next generation of autonomous research systems.

</details>


### [37] [CABS+: Efficient and Scalable Model Merging via Conflict-Aware Sparsification and Adaptive Weight Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12842)
*Yuchen Liu,Zongzhen Yang,Binhang Qi,Hailong Sun,Xiang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CABS+ improves model merging by optimizing task balancing and reducing time complexity through adaptive weight allocation and asymmetric fitness functions, achieving significant performance gains and efficiency improvements over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging is promising for creating multi-task models without retraining, but suffers from parameter conflicts and knowledge interference that degrade performance. Existing methods like CABS have limitations in time complexity (exponential grid search) and optimization biases that favor high-performance tasks over balanced gains.

Method: CABS+ extends CABS with two key improvements: 1) Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA) optimizes merging coefficients via gradient-free search to reduce time complexity, 2) Asymmetric fitness function promotes more comprehensive performance gains across tasks. The paper also introduces Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify model mergeability and guide model selection.

Result: Extensive experiments across 27 datasets and 5 models show CABS+ improves overall performance by 16.97% over AdaMerging and 12.93% over WUDIMerging, with stronger stability/robustness, uses <25% GPU memory of AdaMerging, and achieves nearly 4x speedup in merging time over WUDIMerging.

Conclusion: CABS+ effectively addresses the limitations of previous model merging methods by balancing task performance while reducing computational overhead, making it a more practical and efficient solution for constructing unified multi-task models across diverse architectures and task settings.

Abstract: Model merging has recently attracted significant attention as a promising paradigm for constructing unified multi-task models without requiring additional retraining. However, parameter conflicts and knowledge interference across tasks often degrade merged-model performance. Prior work introduced Conflict-Aware and Balanced Sparsification (CABS), which reduces parameter interference through structured pruning and sequential masking. However, CABS relies on grid search to determine scaling coefficients, resulting in exponential time complexity, while its optimization objective can be dominated by high-performance tasks, leading to suboptimal overall performance. To address these limitations, we extend CABS and propose CABS+. Specifically, Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA) optimizes merging coefficients via a gradient-free search scheme to reduce time complexity, while an asymmetric fitness function promotes more comprehensive performance gains across tasks. Moreover, we conduct a systematic empirical study of key factors influencing model merging performance and propose Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify model mergeability and guide model selection. We compare CABS+ with state-of-the-art model merging methods, including CABS, AdaMerging, and WUDIMerging, across 27 datasets and 5 models covering large language, small-scale language, and vision models. Extensive experiments verify the effectiveness and efficiency of CABS+. Compared with AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, CABS+ improves overall performance by 16.97% and 12.93%, respectively, exhibits stronger stability and robustness across varying task numbers and model architectures, uses less than 25% of the GPU memory required by AdaMerging, and achieves nearly a 4x speedup in merging time over WUDIMerging.

</details>


### [38] [Beyond Retrieval: Query-Conditioned Reuse of Long-Horizon Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12847)
*Yifei Li,Heng Wang,Lingling Zhang,Muye Huang,Xinyu Zhang,Jiashuai Liu,Hang Yan,Rongman Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for evaluating how agents can effectively reuse retrieved trajectories when environment conditions change, with a simple query-conditioned reuse method that outperforms full trajectory injection while using fewer tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieval can identify relevant past trajectories, but doesn't solve how to effectively reuse them when users, entities, constraints, or environment state have changed - creating a bottleneck for long-horizon trajectory memory systems.

Method: Proposed an evaluation framework that isolates the reuse problem from retrieval by holding other factors constant. Introduced Query-Conditioned Reuse (QCR) - a simple target-bound note format containing reusable procedures, bindings, applicability conditions, and verification requirements.

Result: QCR achieved 62.3% average Success across 2,391 instances in WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selected reusable memories for 94.8% of targets, achieving near-oracle performance.

Conclusion: The framework successfully separates retrieval quality from trajectory reuse, showing that direct trajectory injection loses utility as traces grow or conditions change, while target-bound support better preserves gains. QCR demonstrates effective reuse is possible with simple formats.

Abstract: Retrieval can identify a past trajectory that may matter, yet it does not specify how an acting agent should use that trajectory after users, entities, constraints, or environment state have changed. We identify this post-retrieval reuse step as a distinct bottleneck for long-horizon trajectory memory and formulate an evaluation framework that holds candidate retrieval, target state, model, decoding, and tool budget fixed while varying the support delivered to the agent. We instantiate the framework with query-conditioned reuse (QCR), a deliberately simple target-bound note that records a reusable procedure, bindings to recover, applicability conditions, and verification requirements. QCR serves to test the reuse hypothesis rather than to claim a universally preferred memory format. Across 2,391 target instances in WebArena, WorkArena, and AppWorld, QCR reaches 62.3% average Success, 10.7 points above Full Trajectory, while using 48.9% fewer online tokens. Summary reranking selects a reusable memory for 94.8% of targets, placing end-task Success within 1.8 points of an oracle reusable selector. Analyses by trajectory length and source--target binding shift show that direct trajectory injection loses much of its utility as traces grow longer or source-specific values change, whereas target-bound support preserves a larger share of the measured gain. The resulting framework separates retrieval quality from the problem of turning retrieved experience into safe, useful support for a new task.

</details>


### [39] [Practice Makes Unsafe: Skill Misevolution in Self-Improving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12851)
*Xutao Mao,Liangjie Zhao,Xiang Zheng,Cong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-improving LLM agents can create unsafe reusable skills from successful trajectories, leading to skill misevolution where compromised experience causes harmful procedures to persist and spread across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks can't measure risk across the full lifecycle of skill evolution - from authoring to retrieval to later execution. There's a need to understand how unsafe skills propagate through self-improving LLM agents.

Method: The authors introduce SkillMisevo-Gym (a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill state) and SkillMisevo-Bench (a benchmark with malicious exposure to carryover tasks). They also propose SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs reuse.

Result: Across 25 agent-method configurations covering 525 tasks, all 21 evolved configurations authored unsafe artifacts, but only 15 led to fresh-session harm. Three malicious tasks raised carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. SafeEvolve reduced unsafe retrieval by 26.7pp and fresh-session harm by 17.3pp.

Conclusion: Persistent-adaptation safety requires governing both what updates are written and what future executors can reuse. The lifecycle approach reveals critical risks in skill evolution that static benchmarks miss.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents convert successful trajectories into persistent cross-task state. An unsafe success can thereby become reusable policy after its triggering input disappears. Skill evolution makes this failure measurable by distilling operational trajectories into executable, transferable, and inspectable procedures. Because evolution optimizes task outcomes rather than procedure safety, compromised experience can cause skill misevolution. Existing benchmarks measure current behavior or static artifacts but cannot attribute risk across authoring, retrieval, and later execution. To expose this lifecycle, we introduce SkillMisevo-Gym, a lifecycle-aware harness that versions skill state across agent frameworks, and SkillMisevo-Bench, a frozen design from malicious exposure to carryover tasks, with concept-aligned benign tasks and nine lifecycle metrics. We also introduce SafeEvolve, a wrapper that repairs unsafe content and governs subsequent reuse. Across 25 agent-method configurations, each covering 525 tasks in 25 episodes, all 21 evolved configurations author unsafe artifacts, while only fifteen lead to fresh-session harm. In the exposure sweep, three malicious tasks raise carryover ASR from 16.0% to 35.3%. Across representative skill evolution methods, SafeEvolve reduces unsafe retrieval and fresh-session harm by 26.7 and 17.3 percentage points, respectively, while mean benign utility changes by only 0.4 points. Together, persistent-adaptation safety must govern what updates write and what future executors reuse. Code is available at https://github.com/henrymao2004/misevolve.

</details>


### [40] [AI and Consumer Rights in India Working Paper](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12863)
*Omir Kumar,Sriya Sridhar,Vibhav Mithal,Balaraman Ravindran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: India's Consumer Protection Act 2019 may cover AI harms but has significant gaps in addressing the unique challenges of AI systems, particularly regarding causation proof and allocating liability across complex value chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems become widespread in consumer applications, questions about liability for AI-related harms remain unresolved in India. The research aims to assess whether existing consumer protection laws adequately address these emerging challenges.

Method: The paper analyzes India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019 through legal examination to determine its applicability to AI-related incidents, focusing on definitions, liability frameworks, and enforcement mechanisms.

Result: While the Act's broad definitions appear technology-agnostic and could cover AI harms, significant gaps exist: 1) Technical challenges in proving causation between AI defects and harm, 2) The Act's traditional stakeholder roles don't match the overlapping responsibilities in AI value chains, 3) Lack of proportionate mechanisms for complex, multistakeholder AI harms, and 4) Need for clarification on sector-specific enforcement overlaps.

Conclusion: Current liability frameworks under India's Consumer Protection Act lack adequate mechanisms to effectively address AI-related harms. The Act may cover AI entities but requires clarification on enforcement and proportionate allocation of liability across the complex AI value chain to be truly effective.

Abstract: As AI systems proliferate in consumer facing applications, questions about liability for AI related harms remain unresolved. This working paper examines whether India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019, adequately addresses harm caused by defective AI products and services, and whether it proportionately allocates liability across the AI value chain.
  The Act's broad definitions of product liability, harm, and deficiency appear technology agnostic and potentially applicable to AI related incidents including personal injury, psychological harm, biased outputs, and loss of control. However, significant gaps remain. Proving causation between AI defects and consumer harm presents a technical challenge, as AI failures often stem from design choices rather than discrete defects. Additionally, the Act's framework assumes distinct roles for manufacturers, sellers, and service providers, yet the AI value chain involves overlapping responsibilities among data providers, model developers, deployers, and users that do not neatly map to these categories. Current liability frameworks lack proportionate mechanisms to effectively address complex, multistakeholder AI harms. While the Act may cover AI entities, enforcement requires clarification on sector specific overlaps.

</details>


### [41] [ReflectFact: Self-Reflective Agents for Improving Comprehension and Reasoning in Multi-Hop Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12877)
*Runze Zhao,Zixin Tang,Xiaoshuai Hao,Leyuan Chang,Xiaopeng Fu,Boyu Qiao,Dongyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReflectFact is a self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification that addresses comprehension and reasoning defects in existing methods through explicit reasoning path planning, evidence-drift verification, and reasoning reflection verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-hop fact verification is critical for combating misinformation on social media but remains challenging. Current multi-agent collaboration methods have two key limitations: 1) agents may lack awareness of global verification objectives, causing reasoning deviation, and 2) conflicts between parametric knowledge and evidence can undermine evidence-grounded reasoning and lead to incorrect verdicts.

Method: ReflectFact introduces three key tasks: 1) Explicit Reasoning Path Planning - builds evidence-grounded reasoning paths by resolving implicit entities, decomposing claims into sub-questions, and integrating verified facts; 2) Evidence-Drift Verification - makes agents re-answer by quoting supporting evidence when answers echo parametric prior, calibrating evidence deviation; 3) Reasoning Reflection Verification - re-examines each reasoning step and regenerates it when inconsistencies are detected, correcting biases through a global task perspective.

Result: Extensive experiments on HOVER and EX-FEVER datasets demonstrate that ReflectFact effectively remedies comprehension and reasoning defects of existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32% on HOVER and 2.78% on EX-FEVER.

Conclusion: ReflectFact provides a novel self-reflective agent framework that addresses critical limitations in multi-hop fact verification through comprehensive verification mechanisms, leading to more reliable verdicts and superior performance compared to existing approaches.

Abstract: Multi-hop fact verification, which verifies claims by reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence, is critical for combating misinformation on social media yet remains highly challenging. Recent methods primarily rely on multi-agent collaboration to decompose fact verification into specialized subtasks. However, these methods face two critical limitations: (1) agents may perform individual subtasks without sufficient awareness of the global verification objective, causing their reasoning to deviate from the intended direction; and (2) conflicts between parametric knowledge and the provided evidence may undermine evidence-grounded reasoning and lead to incorrect verdicts. To address these challenges, we propose ReflectFact, a novel self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification. ReflectFact introduces three key tasks. Explicit Reasoning Path Planning builds an evidence-grounded reasoning path by resolving implicit entities, decomposing the claim into sub-questions, and integrating the verified facts into a verdict. Evidence-Drift Verification makes the agent re-answer by quoting the supporting evidence when a grounded answer merely echoes its parametric prior, thereby calibrating evidence deviation to ensure grounded comprehension. Reasoning Reflection Verification re-examines each reasoning step and regenerates it once an inconsistency is detected, correcting reasoning flaws such as location bias and replacement bias through a global task perspective. Subsequently, the agent aggregates validated reasoning chains to yield reliable verdicts. Extensive experiments on HOVER and EX-FEVER demonstrate that ReflectFact effectively remedies the comprehension and reasoning defects of existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance and respectively outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.32\% and 2.78\% on the two datasets.

</details>


### [42] [Predictive Memory Localization: Forecasting Selective Intervention Paths from Internal Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12892)
*Jinhao Jing,Tian Zeyu,Lucas Qingyang Fang,Zhisheng Chen,Shuang Chen,Yuhao Luo,Qiannian Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Predictive Memory Localization (PML) treats activation steering intervention paths as predictive objects, enabling selective control with risk-aware decision making through strength-disjoint causal response analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Activation steering uses localized representations for control, but existing methods don't reveal whether directions have selective operating regimes or can predict outcomes at different intervention strengths. There's a need for a framework that makes memory localization falsifiable and enables risk-aware intervention decisions.

Method: PML treats measured-grid intervention paths as predictive objects for memory localization. It separates random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage. The approach compares static localization and supervised geometry using strength-disjoint low-dose causal response analysis across extensive datasets (3,000 records, 9 datasets, 14 domains).

Result: At layer 7, geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction achieves 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6-3.4 percentage points. Responses at |α|=0.1 provide strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths |α|∈{0.25,0.5}. A predictor-driven selector improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage, achieving 0.801-0.828 macro AUROC on held-out records across models.

Conclusion: PML transforms memory localization into a falsifiable forecast of margin-level selective outcomes and enables risk-aware intervention decisions, making activation steering more reliable and predictable while avoiding most evaluations in dense scans.

Abstract: Activation steering turns localized representations into control directions, but localization alone does not reveal whether a direction has a selective operating regime. We introduce Predictive Memory Localization (PML), which treats the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization. PML separates random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage, and compares static localization and supervised geometry with a strength-disjoint low-dose causal response. Our frozen study covers 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains, yielding 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths and 210,000 distinct path-strength evaluations. At layer 7, the geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction reaches 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 percentage points under a record-paired bootstrap. Across record-, dataset-, and domain-grouped splits, responses at $|α|=0.1$ are the strongest signal for outcomes at disjoint strengths $|α|\in\{0.25,0.5\}$. On held-out records, a predictor-driven selector chooses a coefficient or abstains, improves utility and reduces semantic-neighbor damage relative to a train-tuned fixed-strength policy, and avoids most evaluations in a dense scan. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions retain selective-path gains and low-dose responses yield 0.801-0.828 record-held-out macro AUROC. PML therefore turns memory localization into a falsifiable forecast of margin-level selective outcomes and a risk-aware intervention decision.

</details>


### [43] [Polish Medical Visual Question Answering: Vision-Language Models Underutilize Visual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12928)
*Jakub Pokrywka,Łukasz Grzybowski,Antoni Lasik,Marek Kubis,Jeremi Ignacy Kaczmarek,Wojciech Kusa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers create a Polish medical VQA benchmark from certification exams, find current models underperform humans, and show models rely more on text than images for answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need for Polish-language medical visual question answering benchmarks to evaluate vision-language models in specialized medical contexts, using authentic physician certification exam questions.

Method: Built benchmark from Polish Board Certification Examination questions with images spanning multiple medical specialties. Evaluated Polish-oriented, open-weight, and commercial VQA models. Conducted ablation studies by removing images/questions to assess visual grounding and categorized questions by image importance.

Result: Best model achieved 79.0% accuracy on full VQA set, with only GPT-5.6 surpassing approximate human reference on subset with candidate responses. Models derived more information from text than images, performed worse on image-dominant questions, but still achieved above-chance accuracy from answer choices alone.

Conclusion: Polish medical VQA remains challenging with models underperforming humans. Models show limited visual grounding, relying more on textual cues, but can achieve non-trivial performance even with missing key components, suggesting over-reliance on answer choice patterns.

Abstract: We introduce a Polish-language medical visual question answering (VQA) benchmark, built from Polish Board Certification Examination questions for licensed physicians and dentists pursuing specialist certification. The benchmark comprises image-containing questions spanning diverse medical specialties and visual domains, together with a text-only question answering (QA) control set. We evaluate Polish-oriented, general-purpose open-weight, and commercial vision-language models. The task remains challenging: the best model achieves 79.0\% accuracy on the full VQA set, and only GPT-5.6 surpasses the approximate human reference on the subset with available candidate responses; all other evaluated models perform worse than humans. To assess visual grounding, we compare complete inputs with configurations omitting the image, the question, or both, and categorize questions by image importance. Models derive more useful information from the question text than from the image and perform worse on image-dominant questions. Across both QA and VQA, they nevertheless achieve above-chance accuracy from the answer choices alone, showing that non-trivial performance can persist even when key task components are missing.

</details>


### [44] [FlashDrive: Flash Vision-Language-Action Inference for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12932)
*Zekai Li,Yihao Liang,Hongfei Zhang,Jian Chen,Yesheng Liang,Zhijian Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlashDrive accelerates Vision-Language-Action models for autonomous driving by co-designing algorithms and systems to address four inference bottlenecks, achieving 4.7x speedup with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLA models for autonomous driving have excessive computational costs that prevent real-time control, with four cascading bottlenecks: visual encoding waste on overlapping frames, repeated language-model prefill, serial low-entropy reasoning token generation, and inefficient flow-matching denoising.

Method: FlashDrive uses algorithm-system co-design with four specific optimizations: 1) streaming KV-cache reuse across overlapping video frames, 2) non-autoregressive diffusion drafter for speculative decoding of low-entropy driving reasoning, 3) adaptive step caching for velocity field denoising, and 4) CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion at the system level.

Result: On Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization, FlashDrive reduces end-to-end latency from 717ms to 151ms (4.7x speedup) while maintaining accuracy: minADE6@6.4s changes by only 0.08m, minADE1 improves, and closed-loop collision and off-road rates improve in simulation, raising inference frequency from 1.4Hz to 6.6Hz.

Conclusion: FlashDrive demonstrates that simultaneous co-design targeting multiple inference bottlenecks can make large reasoning VLA models practical for real-time autonomous driving, moving end-to-end approaches substantially closer to deployment.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models promise to bring end-to-end reasoning to autonomous driving, but their computational cost remains far too high for real-time control. The core challenge is structural: VLA inference is not a single bottleneck but a cascade of four. Visual encoding wastes compute on overlapping video frames; language-model prefill recomputes context that could be carried over from the previous timestep; reasoning tokens are generated serially despite low entropy; and flow-matching denoising applies uniform compute to a non-uniform velocity field. Addressing any one stage in isolation leaves the others untouched. We propose FlashDrive, an algorithm-system co-design framework that targets all four stages simultaneously. Our key insight is that each bottleneck admits a distinct, lightweight algorithmic shortcut: temporal overlap enables streaming KV-cache reuse across frames; the low per-token entropy and strong intra-block correlations of driving-domain reasoning make a non-autoregressive diffusion drafter highly effective for speculative decoding; and the velocity field's structure---sharp at the endpoints, flat in the middle---permits adaptive step caching that concentrates compute where it matters. Layered on system-level CUDA Graph compilation and kernel fusion, these techniques compound. Applied to Alpamayo 1.5-10B with W4A8 quantization, FlashDrive reduces end-to-end latency from 717ms to 151ms (4.7x) while leaving accuracy essentially unchanged: minADE6@6.4s shifts by only 0.08m, minADE1 improves, and closed-loop collision and off-road rates improve in simulation. By raising a 10B-parameter reasoning VLA from 1.4~Hz to 6.6~Hz on a single GPU, FlashDrive moves end-to-end autonomous driving substantially closer to real-time deployment.

</details>


### [45] [Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, and Fragility in Perturbation Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12935)
*Lei You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DECAF (Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, And Fragility) is a novel perturbation-based attribution method that decomposes model responses into three interpretable components tracking how evidence accumulates along perturbation paths, revealing meaningful behaviors beyond just response magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional perturbation methods only measure the magnitude of model response to input changes, but this doesn't reveal what the response means. The same magnitude can indicate supporting evidence, opposing evidence, or fragile responses that vanish at the endpoint. There's a need to interpret how model contrast develops as inputs are progressively revealed.

Method: DECAF tracks how the contrast between paired inputs (factual and counterfactual) develops as they are progressively revealed. It decomposes responses into three components: E (evidence aligned with final contrast), C (contradiction opposing final contrast), and F (fragility - strong responses along path that vanish at endpoint). The decomposition preserves absolute magnitude (Abs = E + C + F) and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms.

Result: Experiments show DECAF components track independently measured behaviors. In a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit, the largest DECAF component agrees with observed behavior in 96.4% of cases vs 35.0% for magnitude alone. Changing reveal paths increases total response by 80% but evidence barely changes while fragility grows 4x+. DECAF outperforms attribution baselines on FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, and on a 1B-scale DINOv2 model matches strong gradient baselines with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

Conclusion: DECAF provides a principled decomposition of perturbation responses that reveals meaningful model behaviors beyond simple magnitude measurements. It offers interpretable components that track actual model behaviors, is computationally efficient compared to gradient methods, and can identify different types of model responses that would be indistinguishable using traditional perturbation magnitude alone.

Abstract: Perturbation methods explain model decisions by measuring prediction changes under altered inputs, but response magnitude tells us only how much a model reacts, not what that reaction means. The same magnitude can support the final factual-counterfactual difference, oppose it, or arise strongly along the perturbation path yet vanish at the endpoint. We therefore track how the contrast develops as paired inputs are progressively revealed, using the final contrast to interpret the trajectory. We introduce DECAF (Decomposition of Evidence, Contradiction, And Fragility), which routes aligned, opposed, and endpoint-null responses into evidence E, contradiction C, and fragility F. The decomposition preserves ordinary magnitude exactly, Abs = E + C + F, and is unique under endpoint-relative axioms. Across controlled vision and tabular settings, the three components track independently measured behavior. In a 72-model ImageNet-9 audit, we compare cases with nearly identical response magnitude but different independently measured behaviors. The largest DECAF component agrees with an observed behavior in 96.4% of cases, compared with 35.0% for magnitude alone. Changing only the reveal path increases total response by nearly 80%, yet evidence barely changes while fragility grows by more than 4x. On FunnyBirds and ImageNet-1k, short forward-only DECAF trajectories outperform the tested general-purpose attribution baselines. On a 1B-scale DINOv2 model, a short trajectory matches a strong gradient-based baseline with 4.75x lower wall time and 2.36x lower peak memory.

</details>


### [46] [Moose: Latent concept learning with reasoning-shortcut awareness in $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12961)
*Olga Mashkova,Asaad Mohammedsaleh,Fernando Zhapa-Camacho,Robert Hoehndorf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Moose is a neuro-symbolic learning method that compiles OWL EL ontologies to Sentential Decision Diagrams for differentiable reasoning, enabling latent concept learning while avoiding reasoning shortcuts in ontology settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neuro-symbolic methods only work with propositional theories or Datalog, and reasoning-shortcut awareness has not been investigated in ontology settings despite OWL 2 EL's importance in large production ontologies like Gene Ontology and SNOMED CT.

Method: Moose compiles an EL++ TBox and finite ABox to a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD), which serves as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer. It adds closure clauses on declared exhaustive families outside the EL++ profile to handle partial supervision limitations.

Result: The method shows termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial intermediate sizes (validated in Lean). Moose improves over propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology embedding baselines on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo tasks, and presents the first reasoning-shortcut analysis in OWL EL settings.

Conclusion: Moose enables effective latent concept learning over OWL EL ontologies, addressing reasoning-shortcut challenges and demonstrating superior performance compared to existing approaches in ontology-based neuro-symbolic learning.

Abstract: The OWL 2 EL profile is used in some of the largest production ontologies, including the Gene Ontology and SNOMED CT. Existing neuro-symbolic (NeSy) learning methods accept propositional theories or Datalog, and reasoning-shortcut (RS) awareness has not been investigated in ontology settings. We present Moose, a method that compiles an $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ TBox and finite ABox to a Sentential Decision Diagram (SDD). The SDD acts as a differentiable weighted-model-counting layer, and we add closure clauses outside the $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ profile on declared exhaustive families to overcome the limited expressivity of $\mathcal{EL}^{++}$ under partial supervision. We show termination, soundness, completeness, and polynomial intermediate sizes, and validate the proofs in Lean. We then define the first formal partial-supervision latent-concept-learning task over an OWL EL ontology, i.e., learning per-individual classifiers for latent concepts from observed ABox literals, and evaluate Moose on MNIST-with-ontology and Pizzaïolo. Moose improves over propositional-NeSy, fuzzy-logic, and ontology embedding baselines, and presents the first reasoning-shortcut analysis in an OWL EL setting.

</details>


### [47] [Foundations of MT-PDCL: Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13018)
*Costin Bădică,Amelia Bădică*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MT-PDCL is a probabilistic logic programming framework that extends to continuous domains using measure theory and Lebesgue integration, replacing discrete grounding with exact continuous inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current probabilistic logic programming frameworks are limited to finite domains and discrete distributions due to their reliance on grounding logic programs into discrete propositional representations. This restriction prevents exact inference on continuous domains.

Method: Introduces MT-PDCL (Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic) with: 1) stochastic variables over bounded index domains, 2) interpretation spaces equipped with standard Borel σ-algebras, 3) logical variables operating natively over continuous measurable spaces, 4) declarative entailment defined through exact Lebesgue integration, and 5) a continuous immediate consequence operator.

Result: Replaces combinatorial discrete grounding bottleneck with exact, algebraic, and structurally differentiable inference over continuous measure spaces. Achieves expressive power of continuous probabilistic models while preserving pure declarative syntax of definite clause logic.

Conclusion: MT-PDCL provides a foundational framework for probabilistic logic programming that eliminates the finite-domain restriction, enabling exact inference over continuous domains through measure-theoretic foundations and Lebesgue integration, though at the cost of facing the geometric curse of dimensionality rather than combinatorial complexity.

Abstract: Standard probabilistic logic programming frameworks typically rely on grounding logic programs into discrete propositional representations. This operational requirement restricts exact inference to finite domains and discrete probability distributions. In this paper, we introduce Measure-Theoretic Probabilistic Definite Clause Logic (MT-PDCL), a generalized foundational framework that eliminates this finite-domain restriction. By explicitly defining stochastic variables over bounded index domains and equipping the interpretation space with standard Borel $σ$-algebras, MT-PDCL allows logical variables to operate natively over continuous measurable spaces. Building on Continuous Distribution Semantics, MT-PDCL models probabilistic rules as mutually independent causal events. However, rather than aggregating these derivations via finite boolean circuits, declarative entailment is formally defined through exact Lebesgue integration over the continuous measure space. We introduce a continuous immediate consequence operator that unifies the integration of continuous prior distributions with the evaluation of exact continuous observations. We demonstrate that this approach replaces the combinatorial bottleneck of discrete grounding with exact, algebraic, and structurally differentiable inference. While this transition trades discrete combinatorics for the geometric curse of dimensionality, it achieves the expressive power of continuous probabilistic models while preserving the pure declarative syntax of definite clause logic.

</details>


### [48] [From Local Mismatch to Global Impact: Optimizing Cache Reuse Policy for Efficient Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13043)
*Xichen Ye,Yifan Wu,Zhikang Xie,Xiangyu Yue,Cheng Jin,Weizhong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GCache is a novel cache-based acceleration method for diffusion models that uses bilevel optimization to learn cache reuse policies aligned with generation quality, achieving both speedup and quality improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion models suffer from substantial inference overhead despite dominant visual generation performance. Existing cache-based acceleration policies rely on local similarity heuristics that are misaligned with final generation quality due to non-uniform error propagation along the denoising trajectory.

Method: Proposes Global-Impact Cache (GCache): 1) Establishes theoretical characterization of error propagation upper bound, 2) Reparameterizes propagation exponent with Bernstein form, 3) Reformulates cache policy search as bilevel optimization - inner objective finds optimal reuse policy, outer objective aligns error-weighting function with generation quality loss.

Result: GCache consistently outperforms prior caching strategies on video and image generation. On Wan2.1 video diffusion model: maintains 2.17x speedup while significantly enhancing quality - reducing LPIPS from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

Conclusion: GCache effectively reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance by learning to prioritize computation where it most impacts visual fidelity, addressing the limitations of heuristic-based cache policies in diffusion models.

Abstract: Diffusion models have achieved dominant performance in visual generation but suffer from substantial inference overhead. While cache-based acceleration has emerged as a promising solution, existing policies rely on local similarity heuristics, which we identify as being significantly misaligned with final generation quality. This discrepancy stems from the non-uniform propagation and accumulation of errors along the denoising trajectory. To address this, we propose Global-Impact Cache (GCache). We first establish a rigorous theoretical characterization of the error propagation upper bound. Recognizing that this bound can be overly conservative for complex, highly non-convex diffusion models, we further reparameterize the propagation exponent with a Bernstein form and reformulate cache policy search as a bilevel optimization problem. In detail, GCache identifies an optimal reuse policy in the inner objective while aligning the error-weighting function with generation quality loss in the outer objective. This framework effectively reconciles theoretical rigor with empirical performance, learning to prioritize computation where it most impacts visual fidelity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GCache consistently outperforms prior caching strategies on both video and image generation. Notably, on the state-of-the-art Wan2.1 video diffusion model, GCache maintains a 2.17x speedup while significantly enhancing generation quality, reducing LPIPS from 0.1095 to 0.0316.

</details>


### [49] [BoardroomAI: Dependency-Aware Human-Steerable Multi-Agent Deliberation through Evolving Decision Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13046)
*Sanjeev Manivannan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BoardroomAI enables humans to persistently intervene in multi-agent decision-making by allowing real-time modification of assumptions, constraints, evidence, and priorities through a structured decision graph framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional multi-agent systems treat humans as providing only initial input and receiving final output, lacking the ability for ongoing human participation as decisions evolve with changing evidence, constraints, and priorities.

Method: Four-component system: (1) typed decision graph representing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, etc.; (2) intervention compiler converting human actions into graph updates; (3) dependency-aware propagation identifying affected subgraphs and reactivating relevant specialists; (4) evaluation framework measuring intervention impact.

Result: Propagation matched exhaustive impact while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes; selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all unaffected nodes, and produced valid decisions in 6 of 12 cases (abstaining in 6 where context was insufficient).

Conclusion: Human-steered multi-agent deliberation requires decision-sufficient context closure for effective synthesis, and while the system shows promise in selective recomputation and preservation, the abstentions highlight contextual limitations that need addressing.

Abstract: Organizational decisions are co-created while evidence, constraints, and human priorities continue to evolve. In conventional transcript-based multi-agent systems, humans typically provide an initial problem, agents deliberate internally, and the system returns a final response. BoardroomAI instead treats the human as a persistent participant who can intervene by challenging assumptions, modifying constraints, changing priorities, introducing evidence, or redirecting the decision process. We operationalize this human--agent coexistence through four components: (i) a typed decision graph representing evidence, assumptions, constraints, claims, objections, alternatives, risks, decisions, semantic dependencies, and specialist responsibility; (ii) an intervention compiler that converts confirmed human actions into explicit graph updates; (iii) dependency-aware propagation that identifies affected subgraphs, preserves unaffected artifacts, and selectively reactivates relevant specialists; and (iv) an evaluation framework measuring intervention impact, repair coverage, preservation, recomputation, and decision validity. Across 600 generated decision-DAG interventions, propagation matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes. In a 12-case exploratory pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid updated decisions in six cases while abstaining in the remaining six. These abstentions show that correct intervention routing may still provide insufficient context for synthesis, motivating a \emph{decision-sufficient context closure} for human-steered multi-agent deliberation. All results are synthetic and prototype-level.

</details>


### [50] [DMDIntel: Interpreting Large Language Models via Dynamic Mode Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13048)
*Amogh Joshi,Animesh Mukherjee,Sergey Utyuzhnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DMDIntel uses dynamic mode decomposition to make LLM predictions interpretable by attributing input tokens to hidden state patterns, outperforming existing attribution methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need to make LLM predictions in classification tasks interpretable, as current methods lack sufficient explainability for how models arrive at their decisions.

Method: DMDIntel applies dynamic mode decomposition to LLM hidden states to identify prominent patterns (modes), then ranks input tokens based on their projection values onto these modes, creating an interpretable attribution pipeline.

Result: The method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art techniques including principal component analysis, integrated gradients, and SHAP across three datasets and three model families in ranked attribution of input tokens.

Conclusion: DMDIntel provides an effective and interpretable approach to understanding LLM decision-making processes through input token attribution, offering superior performance compared to existing explainability techniques.

Abstract: In this work, we introduce DMDIntel which uses dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) to make the predictions made by LLMs in a classification task interpretable. It develops an input attribution pipeline, that first decomposes the hidden states of an LLM into prominent patterns, also known as modes, and then associates ranks to the input tokens based on the projection values on those modes. Rigorous experiments across three datasets and three model families consistently show that the ranked attribution of input tokens obtained using DMDIntel by far outperforms state-of-the-art techniques such as principal component analysis, integrated gradients and SHAP.

</details>


### [51] [VALG: An Agentic System for ML Theory Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13060)
*Dechen Zhang,Xuan Tang,Xinxiang Yin,Xingwu Chen,Jian Qian,Difan Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VALG is an autonomous agentic system that combines verification, adaptive problem formulation, and graph-structured proof development to automate ML theory research, successfully tackling COLT 2026 open problems with varying degrees of success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to automate the complex, iterative process of ML theory research where problem formulation, theorem targets, and proof mechanisms must be developed together. Current ML theory requires researchers to formulate hypotheses, test them through analysis, and refine assumptions and proofs - a process that could potentially be organized as an autonomous workflow.
