The final topology is exported to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs, enabling executable reachability and allow/deny tests. Because no public benchmark exists for this requirement-to-topology task, we construct an evaluation set from reference security architecture diagrams. The retrieval set contains 22 templates and 44 synthetic intents across five scenarios, while the held-out set contains 7 templates and 14 intents from finance and government scenarios excluded from retrieval. On the held-out set, additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in fewer than 1.5 rounds on average, and one feedback round raises the post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

</details>


### [75] [Jointly Predicting Courses and Grades Using a Transformer-Based Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13409)
*Paul Savala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE: A transformer model that jointly predicts both the set of courses students will take and their corresponding grades for upcoming semesters, achieving 50% error reduction compared to grade-only prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing predictive models treat student academic history as simple sequences, overlooking concurrent courses within semesters, which leads to inaccurate performance predictions especially for students with heavy or challenging course loads.

Method: TRACE (TRansformer for Academic Course-grade Estimation) encodes courses per semester to capture concurrency effects, uses a novel loss function combining course-set and grade prediction, and incorporates student attribute data naturally.

Result: The joint prediction model reduces mean absolute error by nearly 50% compared to identical architecture predicting grades alone, outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequential models and graph neural network approaches.

Conclusion: Modern neural architectures enable interpretable models adaptable to new institutions via retraining, demonstrating the importance of predicting courses taken during training for early detection systems in higher education.

Abstract: Existing predictive models in learning analytics often treat student academic history as a simple sequence, overlooking the concurrent nature of courses taken within a semester. This simplification can lead to inaccurate performance predictions, particularly for students with heavy or challenging course loads. This paper introduces a TRansformer for Academic Course-grade Estimation (TRACE) that addresses this limitation by jointly predicting both the set of courses a student will take and their corresponding grades for an upcoming semester. Our approach encodes courses on a per-semester basis to capture the effects of course concurrency and utilizes a novel loss function combining course-set prediction with grade prediction. We demonstrate that predicting courses taken in addition to the grades in those courses leads to significant improvements in prediction quality. Trained on ten years of institutional data, our joint prediction model reduces mean absolute error by nearly 50% compared to an identical architecture that predicts grades alone. The model also outperforms traditional LSTM-based sequential models, as well as graph neural network-based approaches, and offers natural ways to incorporate student attribute data. This work demonstrates the utility of modern neural architectures for creating interpretable models that can be adapted to new institutions via retraining and recalibration, as well as the importance of key techniques, such as predicting courses taken during training. We discuss how this model could be incorporated into early detection systems at institutions of higher education.

</details>


### [76] [Who Speaks Matters: Authority-Aware Multi-View RAG over Italian Parliamentary Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410)
*Mirko Tritella,Riccardo Pozzi,Matteo Palmonari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ParliamentRAG is a retrieval-augmented generation system for parliamentary proceedings that addresses risks like speaker dominance and citation misattribution through a novel topic-dependent authority model, outperforming Google NotebookLM in political group coverage and quotation faithfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parliamentary proceedings are voluminous and fragmented, making multi-perspective access difficult. Applying standard RAG to parliamentary transcripts risks: 1) dominance of most frequent speakers, 2) inability to weight speakers by topical expertise, and 3) citation misattribution in politically sensitive text.

Method: Developed ParliamentRAG with a topic-dependent authority model that estimates speaker authority based on query context, combining interpretable components (profession, education, previous interventions). The system retrieves speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates summaries with supporting quotations.

Result: Evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics using automated metrics and blind A/B evaluation by six domain experts. ParliamentRAG achieved higher political group coverage (0.97 vs 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions.

Conclusion: ParliamentRAG successfully addresses the identified risks in parliamentary RAG systems through its topic-dependent authority model, demonstrating improved performance in representing diverse political perspectives while maintaining quotation accuracy, though NotebookLM remains better on prose-oriented dimensions.

Abstract: Parliamentary proceedings are a primary record of democratic deliberation, yet their volume and fragmentation make multi-perspective access difficult for citizens, journalists, and researchers. Applying Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) to parliamentary transcripts introduces three specific risks: dominance of the most frequent speakers, inability to weight speakers according to topical expertise, and citation misattribution in politically sensitive text. We present ParliamentRAG, a RAG system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that addresses these risks jointly. Its core contribution is a topic-dependent authority model that estimates each speaker's authority as a function of the current query, combining interpretable components such as profession, education, and previous interventions. Given a user query, the system retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates a summary synthesizing their perspectives, accompanied by supporting quotations. ParliamentRAG is evaluated against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics via a two-level protocol combining automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts. The system achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, while NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.

</details>


### [77] [Beyond Final Scores: A Systematic Evaluation of Agents for Long-Horizon AI Research and Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13417)
*Yiwei Li,Wanli Yang,Hexiang Tan,Xiangzhou Huang,Zhengyu Chen,Ziran Li,Borun Chen,Shanglin Lei,Huaisheng Zhu,Hao Tian,Fei Sun,Xunliang Cai,Jingang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systematic evaluation framework reveals that current frontier AI models function more like engineering optimizers than autonomous researchers, showing capability in practical solution implementation but limited methodological novelty, with performance shaped by process bottlenecks, experience reuse patterns, and harness design factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of autonomous agents focus too much on final scores, which don't reveal where progress is gained/lost or whether accumulated experience improves later decisions. There's a need for systematic evaluation that characterizes within-run behavior and assesses experience reuse to understand the true capabilities of frontier AI models in long-horizon experimentation tasks.

Method: Developed a new evaluation framework using rule-based metrics to analyze within-run behavior through three dimensions: Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control. Applied controlled comparisons to assess experience reuse within and across tasks. Evaluated seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks using this systematic approach.

Result: Current agents operate like engineering optimizers rather than fully autonomous researchers: they can formulate and implement practical solutions, but performance varies substantially across runs, their strongest solutions mainly adapt/combine established techniques, and genuine methodological novelty remains rare. Performance is shaped by multiple factors including distinct process bottlenecks, experience reuse patterns (which can help or mislead), and harness designs affecting stability.

Conclusion: The findings provide concrete directions for improving model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design. The systematic evaluation framework reveals important insights about AI capabilities that go beyond final scores, showing where progress is made and lost in long-horizon experimentation tasks.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of improving models, systems, and other technical artifacts through long-horizon experimentation. To understand the current state of this capability, however, evaluation must go beyond final scores, which neither reveal where progress is gained or lost nor indicate whether accumulated experience improves later decisions. We therefore present a systematic evaluation of seven frontier models on 36 long-horizon tasks based on a new framework that uses rule-based metrics to characterize within-run behavior through Solution Framing, Execution, and Feedback Control and controlled comparisons to assess experience reuse within and across tasks. The results show that current agents operate more like engineering optimizers than fully autonomous researchers: they can formulate and implement practical solutions, but their performance varies substantially across runs, their strongest solutions mainly adapt or combine established techniques, and genuine methodological novelty remains rare. Detailed analysis reveals that observed performance is shaped by multiple factors, including distinct process bottlenecks behind similar final outcomes, experience reuse that can help or mislead subsequent decisions, and harness designs that affect performance stability. These findings suggest concrete directions for improving model training, inference-time strategies, experience management, and harness design.

</details>


### [78] [Enhancing Virtual Agents through SLMs and Edge-Computing: An Exploratory Evaluation of Think and Memory Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13420)
*Aimilios Hadjiliasi,Louis Nisiotis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores using Small Language Models (SLMs) to implement cognitive components of embodied virtual agents at the edge, focusing on memory and thinking processes for efficient, contextual operation in immersive virtual worlds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implementing cognitively capable embodied agents in complex virtual/Metaverse environments is challenging. There's a need for efficient, edge-based cognitive architectures that can operate contextually in interactive virtual worlds while leveraging recent advances in edge computing and generative AI.

Method: Developed an edge-based virtual agent gateway system on NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using Qwen2.5 SLMs of different sizes. The system implements selected Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) components, focusing on "Think" and "Memory" processes. Conducted simulation experiments evaluating routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency.

Result: Demonstrated an SLM-driven prototype agent system that partially implements CEAA processes. The system showed capability to process service requests and handle memory-driven conversations efficiently at the edge, supporting development of embodied agents with cognitive components operating contextually in immersive environments.

Conclusion: SLMs can effectively support edge-based operation of cognitive agent components, enabling efficient and contextual operation of embodied agents' cognitive "brains" for interactive experiences in virtual worlds. This approach addresses challenges in implementing cognitively capable agents in complex virtual environments.

Abstract: Embodied intelligent virtual agents are expected to operate as persistent, adaptive, and context-aware entities within complex virtual and Metaverse worlds. However, implementing cognitively capable agents in such environments is conceptually and technologically challenging. Among a range of blueprints and development approaches, the Cognitive Embodied Agent Architecture (CEAA) has been developed as an implementation-oriented framework for architecting components of perception, memory, reasoning, planning, and embodied action. Considering the recent advances in edge computing and generative AI language models, this paper explores the use of Small Language Models (SLMs) to support edge-based operation of selected CEAA components, focusing on "Think" and "Memory" as processes central to cognitive orchestration and persistence of virtual agents in interactive virtual worlds. An edge-based virtual agent gateway system was developed and evaluated on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX using Qwen2.5 models of different sizes, exploring the system's capability to process service requests and handle memory-driven conversations. A series of simulation experiments evaluated routing accuracy, memory-read performance, and latency, demonstrating an SLM-driven prototype agent system that partially implements selected CEAA processes to support the development of embodied agents whose cognitive "brain" can operate efficiently and contextually for interactive experiences in immersive virtual worlds.

</details>


### [79] [RAIL: An Automatic Classifier of the Artificial Intelligence Readiness Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13428)
*Juan Irving Vasquez,Juan Terven,Laura-Ivoone Garay-Jimenez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified AI readiness framework (AIRL) with 9-level ordinal scale and RAIL assessment method using specialized LLM agents for automated technology maturity evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI technology readiness frameworks are heterogeneous and difficult to apply automatically: TRL adaptations lack AI-specific criteria, ML-TRL requires internal process artifacts, and dimension models have incomparable scales, creating challenges for investment decisions and project management.

Method: Two contributions: 1) Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL) - a 9-level ordinal scale with environmental evidence ladder, dimensional caps (specification, data existence/quality/legality, expert knowledge, algorithmic maturity), generality-anchoring rule, and assignment disciplines; 2) RAIL (Readiness Assessment via Independent LLM-experts) - panel-of-experts classifier with one evidence agent and six dimension agents (specialized LLMs) whose verdicts are aggregated by deterministic minimum rule and reviewed by chief expert with asymmetric authority (can only confirm or lower, never raise above caps).

Result: The method was tested on several research works, showing consistency and avoiding overestimation that occurs with monolithic LLM classifiers, demonstrating practical applicability and reliability.

Conclusion: The proposed AIRL framework and RAIL assessment method provide a unified, automated approach to AI technology maturity evaluation that addresses limitations of existing frameworks and enables consistent, objective readiness assessment from natural-language descriptions alone.

Abstract: Assessing the maturity of artificial intelligence technologies is essential for investment decisions, project management, and policy monitoring, yet the available readiness frameworks are heterogeneous and difficult to apply automatically: the adaptation of Technology Readiness Levels to AI lacks AI-specific gating criteria, the Machine Learning Technology Readiness Levels presuppose access to internal process artifacts, and AI/data readiness dimension models employ scales that resist direct comparison. This paper makes two contributions. First, we unify these three frameworks into the Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL), a nine-level ordinal scale built on an environmental evidence ladder and complemented by dimensional caps (covering specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, and algorithmic maturity) together with a generality-anchoring rule and explicit assignment disciplines, so that a readiness level becomes decidable from a natural-language description of the work alone. Second, we propose RAIL (Readiness Assessment via Independent LLM-experts), a panel-of-experts classifier that operationalizes the scale: one evidence agent and six independent dimension agents, each a large language model with a narrowly scoped mandate, deliver verdicts that a deterministic minimum rule aggregates and a chief expert reviews under asymmetric authority, confirming or lowering the panel's recommendation but never raising it above the caps. The method was tested in the analysis of several research works showing consistency and avoiding overestimation from monolithic LLM classifiers.

</details>


### [80] [Academic League of Artificial Intelligence - An Integrative Perspective of Teaching, Research, and Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13447)
*Alison R. Panisson,Maria Eduarda W. M. Vianna,Italo Firmino da Silva,Heitor Henrique da Silva,Rafaela Fernandes Savaris,Bernardo Pandolfi Costa,Martin Augusto Gagliotti Vigil,Jim Lau,Agenor Hentz,Andréa Sabedra Bordin,Alexandre Leopoldo Gonçalves,Roberto Rodrigues-Filho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A student-centered, project-based framework for academic leagues that integrates teaching, research, and university extension through democratic governance and collaborative learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Academic leagues have become important for promoting extracurricular education and strengthening university-society integration, but need effective organizational frameworks to combine teaching, research, and extension activities.

Method: Proposes an organizational framework combining democratic governance, collaborative learning, and dynamic project organization through student-centered, project-based approach with initiatives like competition teams, study groups, lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI applications.

Result: The framework successfully fosters technical and transversal competencies through diverse educational, scientific, and extension activities that promote leadership, scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation.

Conclusion: The framework provides a flexible and replicable model for integrating university pillars into engineering/computing education, offering practical guidance for academic leagues and similar student organizations.

Abstract: Academic leagues have become important mechanisms for promoting extracurricular education and strengthening the integration between universities and society. This paper presents the organizational framework adopted by the Academic League of Artificial Intelligence (LIA) at the Federal University of Santa Catarina (UFSC), designed to integrate teaching, research, and university extension through a student-centered, project-based approach. The framework combines democratic governance, collaborative learning, and dynamic project organization to foster both technical and transversal competencies. The framework is illustrated through representative initiatives, including competition teams, study groups, open lectures, knowledge repositories, and AI-powered applications with social impact. These projects demonstrate how diverse educational, scientific, and extension activities can be developed within a common organizational structure while promoting leadership, scientific production, community engagement, and knowledge preservation. The reported experience indicates that the proposed framework provides a flexible and replicable model for integrating the three university pillars into engineering and computing education, offering practical guidance for academic leagues and similar student organizations.

</details>


### [81] [A Unifying Perspective on Causal World Models: From Observations to Representations to Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13456)
*Avinash Kori,Fabrizio Russo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a causal perspective on World Models, defining Causal World Models that capture not just generative capabilities but also entity properties, interactions, and causal structure to support better reasoning and decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current World Models often lack causal understanding - they can generate predictions but don't capture the underlying entity properties, interactions, and causal mechanisms that explain why things happen. This limits their ability to support intelligent decision-making and reasoning beyond training data.

Method: The paper approaches World Models from a causal perspective across multiple abstraction levels, providing a formal definition of Causal World Models. It connects world modeling with causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, and structural causal models to create models that capture entity properties and interactions.

Result: The paper establishes a theoretical framework for Causal World Models that can capture not just what happens but why it happens, through entity properties and interactions. It also addresses identifiability - clarifying when and to what extent the components of a World Model can be recovered from data.

Conclusion: World Models need to incorporate causal structure to truly support intelligent agents. Causal World Models provide a foundation for capturing the underlying mechanisms of environments, enabling better causal reasoning and more informed decision-making beyond mere predictive capabilities.

Abstract: World Models (WM) are increasingly seen as a foundation for intelligent agents that can predict, plan, and act beyond their training distribution. In this paper, we study WMs from a causal perspective across multiple levels of abstraction, ranging from perceptual observations to building a conceptual representation of the structure governing the environment dynamics. We argue that useful WMs must go beyond generative capabilities alone: they should also capture entity properties, entity-to-entity interactions, and entity-to-environment interactions that determine and explain the dynamics of a system. We provide a formal definition of Causal WMs (CWMs) grounded in the tasks they are intended to support, connecting world modelling with existing work in causal representation learning, object-centric learning, causal discovery, structural causal models, and model-based decision-making. Finally, we relate CWMs to the literature on identifiability, clarifying when the components of a WM can be recovered from data and up to which equivalence. With this, we ground WMs in representations and structures that support causal reasoning and informed decision-making.

</details>


### [82] [MARC v1: An Open-Source Multi-Agent Framework for Clinical AI Reasoning and Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13476)
*Saisha Shetty,Satvik Tripathi,Austin Lin,Colin Zhao,Theodore Kim,Don Enwerem,Jacinta Arnold,Shahriar Faghani,Tessa S Cook*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MARC is an open-source multi-agent framework for clinical reasoning that replaces monolithic LLM prompting with deterministic orchestration of specialized agents, featuring automated prompt generation and full configurability via YAML.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the limitations of monolithic LLM prompting for clinical reasoning by seeking to improve reliability, interpretability, and accessibility through structured multi-agent systems.

Method: MARC coordinates role-specialized agents (extraction, reasoning, answer generation, evaluation) with explicit context passing and traceable outputs. It includes a Decomposer module that automatically generates task-specific agent prompts from plain-language descriptions, eliminating manual prompt engineering. The framework supports both API and local deployments and is fully configurable via YAML.

Result: The framework provides a working, open-source system (available on GitHub) that enables stage-wise failure attribution, improved interpretability, and accessibility for clinical domain experts without programming expertise. It is model-agnostic and designed for practical clinical deployment.

Conclusion: MARC represents a shift from monolithic LLM prompting to deterministic multi-agent orchestration for clinical reasoning, offering improved reliability, interpretability, and accessibility while eliminating the need for manual prompt engineering through its automated Decomposer module.

Abstract: We present Multi-Agent Reasoning and Coordination (MARC), an open-source framework that replaces monolithic LLM prompting with deterministic multi-agent orchestration for clinical reasoning. MARC coordinates role-specialized agents for extraction, reasoning, answer generation, and evaluation, with explicit context passing and traceable intermediate outputs, enabling stage-wise failure attribution. We additionally introduce a Decomposer module that generates task-specific agent prompts from a plain-language description, eliminating manual prompt engineering. The framework supports both API-based and local CPU-compatible deployments and is entirely configurable via YAML, without code modifications. MARC is designed to be model-agnostic, interpretable, and accessible to clinical domain experts without programming expertise. The full framework is available at https://github.com/Penn-RAIL/MARC-v1.

</details>


### [83] [AlayaWorld: Interactive Long-Horizon World Modeling - Full Technical Report (v1.1)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13492)
*AlayaWorld Team,Kaipeng Zhang,Chuanhao Li,Yifan Zhan,Yongtao Ge,Yuanyang Yin,Jiaming Tan,Kang He,Liaoyuan Fan,Mingliang Zhai,Ruicong Liu,Xiaojie Xu,Xuangeng Chu,Zhen Li,Zhengyuan Lin,Zhixiang Wang,Zian Meng,Zihui Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Improved version of AlayaWorld with redesigned conditioning signal representation and integration to better match generated content in latent space and temporal structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous conditioning signal representation didn't sufficiently match the generated content in latent representation and temporal structure. The authors aim to create conditioning signals that align more closely with the generated video content.

Method: Made two major architectural changes: 1) Replaced depth-warping-based spatial memory with streaming 3D point-cache renderer; 2) Redesigned conditioning pipeline to encode visual conditions in the same causal-VAE latent space with consistent temporal statistics. Introduced six specific modifications including motion-aware latent conditioning, causal encoding of spatial memory, temporal alignment, hard memory dropout, unified VAE protocols, and removal of camera AdaLN branch.

Result: A substantially revised AlayaWorld system with improved conditioning signal integration that better matches generated content in both latent representation and temporal structure. The new design follows the principle that conditioning signals should closely align with the generated content.

Conclusion: The improved AlayaWorld achieves better alignment between conditioning signals and generated video content through redesigned spatial memory representation and conditioning pipeline, resulting in more coherent video generation with conditioning signals that are better integrated into the model's latent space.

Abstract: This report presents an improved version of AlayaWorld. While the backbone architecture, chunk-wise autoregressive generation scheme, and training data remain unchanged from the previous release, we substantially revise how conditioning signals are represented and integrated into the model. The new design is guided by a simple principle: conditioning signals should match the generated content as closely as possible in both latent representation and temporal structure. To this end, we make two major changes. First, we replace the previous depth-warping-based spatial memory with a streaming 3D point-cache renderer. Second, we redesign the conditioning pipeline so that visual conditions are encoded in the same causal-VAE latent space, with temporal statistics consistent with those of the generated video. Concretely, the new version introduces six modifications: (1) replacing static-frame image conditioning with motion-aware latent conditioning; (2) causally encoding re-rendered spatial memory as a continuous sequence; (3) aligning the temporal-memory window in pixel space; (4) adopting hard memory dropout that removes memory tokens rather than zeroing them; (5) unifying the VAE encoding and decoding protocol across training and inference; and (6) removing the camera AdaLN branch, such that viewpoint control is provided entirely through the re-rendered spatial condition.

</details>


### [84] [QuoteBench: How Matched Scores Can Hide Command-Path Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13547)
*Shangao Li,Yao Zhang,Volker Tresp,Yuanyuan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuoteBench is a benchmark that measures how LLM coding agents' performance degrades when command outputs are serialized and reparsed, showing that much of the failure comes from post-generation processing issues rather than the models themselves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of LLM coding agents only measure matched execution scores, which cannot distinguish between command-generation errors and failures introduced after generation through serialization, wrapping, and reparsing in deployment interfaces.

Method: Created QuoteBench with 56 one-shot tasks from 14 incident-derived families, designed to cross the generation contract with execution transport using one deliberately unescaped added parser. Measures exact final-state validation and tests different configurations to isolate where failures occur.

Result: Replaying the same reply through an added parser lowers success by 55.4-73.2 percentage points; disclosing the parser boundary recovers 30.4-60.7 points for most configurations. GPT-5.6-sol's matched gap of -3.6 points hides -64.3 points of parser damage and +60.7 points of compensation. Deployment configurations can reorder model rankings.

Conclusion: Evaluations of command-issuing agents should report model configuration, generation contract, execution path, operating point, and final-state validator rather than treating matched scores as intrinsic model properties, since boundary adaptation is what separates models at the performance frontier.

Abstract: LLM coding agents issue Bash commands through interfaces that may serialize, wrap, and reparse model output. Matched execution scores alone cannot distinguish command-generation errors from failures introduced after generation. QuoteBench measures this boundary with exact final-state validation on 56 one-shot tasks from 14 incident-derived families, crossing the generation contract with the execution transport around one deliberately unescaped added parser. Escaping at the interpolation point reproduces each replayed reply's raw-path outcome, so any recovery under a disclosed boundary must come from the model changing its generation. Across eight same-window configurations, replaying the same reply through the added parser lowers success by 55.4 to 73.2 percentage points; disclosure recovers 30.4 to 60.7 points for six configurations, and zero or slightly negative for the other two. Raw generation is nearly saturated at the frontier; boundary adaptation is what still separates models. GPT-5.6-sol's matched gap of -3.6 points hides -64.3 points of damage and +60.7 points of compensation. The deployment configuration reorders models: one reversal among 26 comparable pairs is unambiguous and four more sit on single-task margins. Evaluations of command-issuing agents should report the model configuration, generation contract, execution path, operating point, and final-state validator rather than treat a matched score as an intrinsic model property.

</details>


### [85] [OmniScientist: An Omni-Modal Omni-Discipline AI Scientist](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13558)
*Bobo Li,Hao Fei,Tianjie Ju,Mong-Li Lee,Wynne Hsu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniScientist is an end-to-end, omni-modal AI scientist that conducts multidisciplinary research directly from heterogeneous raw evidence using perception and three autonomous agents for ideation, experimentation, and writeup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current foundation models have automated research workflows but lack access to scientifically decisive evidence types (spatial, temporal, cross-channel, procedural relations). Existing systems only reason over text, code, labels, or precomputed summaries, missing crucial evidence needed for genuine scientific discovery.

Method: OmniScientist uses a perception layer and three autonomous agents (ideation, experiment, writeup) in a deterministic pipeline. It enforces novelty screening, statistical validity, execution provenance, and numerical traceability through code-based checks. The system directly processes heterogeneous raw evidence across multiple modalities.

Result: Tested on 36 real-data cases spanning 5 discipline families and 4 evidence families, OmniScientist completed full research from raw data to compiled manuscript in all cases, achieving mean paper score of 6.3 with reference reasoning backbone. In paired comparisons, direct perception improved all 7 evaluation dimensions and won 85% of head-to-head judgments.

Conclusion: Lifecycle-wide perception is essential for evidence-grounded scientific discovery and provides a practical path toward broadly capable AI scientists, demonstrating that direct access to heterogeneous raw evidence significantly improves AI-driven research capabilities.

Abstract: Recent advances in foundation models have enabled AI scientists to automate increasingly complete research workflows, from hypothesis generation and code execution to manuscript preparation. Yet workflow coverage alone does not provide access to the full evidence on which scientific discovery depends. Existing systems typically reason over text, code, labels, or precomputed summaries, leaving scientifically decisive spatial, temporal, cross-channel, and procedural relations unavailable to the agent. We introduce OmniScientist, an end-to-end, omni-modal AI scientist that conducts multidisciplinary research directly from heterogeneous raw evidence. A perception layer and 3 autonomous agents for ideation, experiment, and writeup operate within a deterministic pipeline, allowing observations to shape research questions, experimental decisions, and final claims throughout the research lifecycle. By running idea, rigour, and claim checks in code, the system enforces novelty screening, statistical validity, execution provenance, and numerical traceability. We evaluate OmniScientist on 36 real-data cases spanning 5 discipline families, 4 families of scientific evidence, and modalities including images, signals, audio, video, 3-D structures, trajectories, tables, formulae, and graphs. The system completes the full path from raw data to a compiled manuscript in all 36 cases and achieves a mean overall paper score of 6.3 with the reference reasoning backbone. In paired comparisons against a blind variant that receives only precomputed scalar features, direct perception improves all 7 evaluation dimensions and wins 85% of head-to-head judgments. These results show that lifecycle-wide perception is essential for evidence-grounded scientific discovery and provides a practical path toward broadly capable AI scientists.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [86] [LoKiFormer: Locality-aware Attention with Decoupled Knowledge Memory for Efficient Large Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12419)
*Qiuwu Chen,Zimo Liu,Yuchen Li,Ying Sun,Yifan Zhang,Zhijie Qiu,Zeng You,Ryan Dong,Simeng Ma,Yaofo Chen,Mingkui Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoKiFormer is a new LLM architecture that adds Local Fusion Attention (convolutional fusion for local patterns) and Knowledge Memory Module (explicit global knowledge storage) to improve pre-training efficiency by 1.33x faster convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs have two main inefficiencies: self-attention lacks explicit locality inductive bias (redundantly models local information), and mixture-of-experts implicitly couples knowledge storage with computation paths (hinders flexible global knowledge access).

Method: Augments standard decoder with two modules: (1) Local Fusion Attention (LFA) incorporates convolutional fusion into attention to explicitly capture local patterns, (2) Knowledge Memory Module (KMM) introduces parametric key-value memory that explicitly stores global knowledge in addressable slots, decoupling storage from computation.

Result: Experimental results show LoKiFormer converges 1.33x faster in pre-training than baseline models, demonstrating superior efficiency and effectiveness in integrating both local and global information.

Conclusion: LoKiFormer's explicit handling of local patterns via LFA and global knowledge via KMM addresses key LLM architecture limitations, resulting in significantly faster pre-training convergence while maintaining strong performance.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable breakthroughs across various applications. However, their architectures remain inefficient in pretraining due to two main limitations: (i) self-attention lacks an explicit inductive bias for locality, leading to redundant modeling of sequence-internal local information; (ii) mixture-of-experts (MoE) implicitly couples knowledge storage with computational pathways, hindering flexible access to sequence-external global knowledge. To overcome these limitations, we propose LoKiFormer, a novel LLM architecture that augments the standard decoder with two dedicated modules: 1) Local Fusion Attention (LFA), which incorporates a convolutional fusion to attention, explicitly capturing local patterns and allowing the attention to operate on more informative representations; 2) Knowledge Memory Module (KMM), which introduces a parametric key-value memory that explicitly stores global knowledge in addressable slots, decoupling storage from computation and enabling direct knowledge retrieval. Together, these modules enable LoKiFormer to achieve more efficient and effective integration of information at both levels. Experimental results show that LoKiFormer converges 1.33x faster in pre-training than baseline models, underscoring its superiority over existing LLM architectures.

</details>


### [87] [Multi-AUV Ad-hoc network-based Target Tracking: A Value Gradient Guidance Multi-Agent Diffusion Reinforcement Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12436)
*Jiaao Ma,Chuan Lin,Guangjie Han,Shengchao Zhu,Qian Zhu,Ying Liu,Zhenyu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VGG-MADiffRL algorithm improves multi-AUV target tracking by combining diffusion policies with value gradient guidance in a hierarchical control architecture for stable, accurate underwater coordination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-AUV target tracking faces challenges with acoustic communication constraints, dynamic network topology, and ocean disturbances. Existing MARL methods suffer from high-dimensional state-action modeling, noise sensitivity, unstable training, and degraded tracking performance.

Method: Proposes VGG-MADiffRL (value-gradient-guided multi-agent diffusion RL) and MDCA (diffusion-based hierarchical control architecture). MDCA has three layers: global intelligent control, local online training, and physical action execution. VGG-MADiffRL uses diffusion policies with value gradients to guide action generation, twin value networks with joint optimization and soft target updates.

Result: Experimental results show VGG-MADiffRL achieves faster convergence, higher tracking accuracy, and smoother training dynamics in cooperative tracking scenarios compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: The proposed approach effectively addresses limitations of existing MARL methods for multi-AUV target tracking, demonstrating practical engineering value for dynamic underwater environments with constrained communication and uncertain disturbances.

Abstract: Multi-AUV ad-hoc network-based target tracking requires networked autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) to cooperatively track maneuvering targets under constrained acoustic communication, dynamic topology, and uncertain ocean disturbances. Although multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) enables decentralized coordination through centralized training, existing methods suffer from high-dimensional joint state-action modeling, noise-sensitive policy generation, leading to unstable training and degraded tracking. To address these issues, we propose VGG-MADiffRL, a value-gradient-guided multi-agent diffusion RL algorithm, and MDCA, a diffusion?based hierarchical control architecture. Leveraging underwater mission characteristics, we model sonar detection mechanisms and ocean current disturbances, formulating cooperative tracking for multi-AUV ad-hoc networks as an MDP. The proposed MDCA constitutes a three-tier closed-loop control framework: a global intelligent control layer, a local online training layer, and a physical action execution layer. This structure enables synergistic optimization across task allocation, local decision processes, and execution feedback. Within MDCA, the local online training layer is the policy learning framework; VGG-MADiffRL builds on diffusion policies and incorporates value gradients to guide action generation in the reverse denoising process, steering the generated actions towards higher expected returns. It employs twin value networks with joint optimization and soft target updates to mitigate overestimation and training oscillations, promoting more stable convergence. Experimental results show that VGG-MADiffRL consistently achieves faster convergence, higher tracking accuracy, and smoother training dynamics in cooperative tracking scenarios, validating its effectiveness and practical engineering value in dynamic underwater settings.

</details>


### [88] [Which Site, and When: A Free-Satellite-Data Test of Himalayan Glacial Lake Bursts, Landslides, and Ice Floods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12422)
*Matthew Kahn,Milan Arjel,Nirmala Adhikari,Mingmar Sherpa,James Pope*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers develop predictive models for glacial lake outburst floods in Nepal using free satellite data, achieving ROC scores of 0.73-0.83 for timing predictions, but find terrain-based susceptibility predictions are less reliable than expected when properly evaluated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous feasibility studies showed that satellite data (radar interferometry for deformation and weather data for stress periods) can identify glacial lake hazards but lacked predictive models. There's a need for models that can predict both which sites are susceptible to outbursts and when triggers will occur.

Method: The study uses free satellite data to develop models for three hazards: large moraine-/ice-dammed bursts, rainfall-triggered landslides, and smaller glacier pond floods. Each hazard is addressed with two separate questions (susceptibility and timing). The approach uses 589 dated outbursts from HMAGLOFDB and thousands of landslides, matching events against similar unfailed sites. Models are evaluated with spatial cross-validation that withholds entire map tiles to prevent geographic overfitting.

Result: Antecedent weather predicts trigger timing with ROC scores of 0.73 for big bursts, 0.83 for landslides, and 0.82 for small floods. Terrain-based susceptibility predictions appear strong (~0.9) when naively evaluated but drop to 0.76, 0.71, and 0.54 (no better than chance) when properly matched against comparable nearby sites. Simple gradient-boosted models perform similarly to five deep-learning approaches, with the baseline winning outright for lake hazards. A simple three-rule decision tree on ruggedness and monsoon rainfall reproduces the baseline performance.

Conclusion: Free satellite data can effectively predict the timing of glacial lake outburst triggers but has more limited value for assessing susceptibility when properly evaluated. Simple models perform as well as complex deep-learning approaches. The study provides a ranked Nepal watchlist as a prioritization aid (not a prediction tool) and identifies the limits of free data for this application.

Abstract: Two free satellite signals carry real information about glacial-lake outburst risk in the Nepal Himalaya: radar interferometry sees a moraine dam slowly sagging, and satellite weather marks the weeks when a primed lake is under stress. A companion feasibility study found that deformation indicates which lake is destabilizing and weather indicates when it is at risk, but proposed no predictive model. To address this gap, we propose and evaluate models that predict which site is susceptible and when a trigger arrives. We test three related hazards on free data alone: large moraine- and ice-dammed bursts, rainfall-triggered landslides, and smaller floods from ponds on and around a glacier. Each hazard gets two questions, never blended. Using 589 dated outbursts from HMAGLOFDB and several thousand catalogued landslides, we match each event against similar but unfailed sites, and hold every model to a strong simple baseline under spatial cross-validation that withholds whole map tiles, so no model succeeds by recognising a trained-on neighbourhood. Antecedent weather times the trigger at ROC 0.73 for big bursts, 0.83 for landslides, and 0.82 for small floods. Terrain ranks susceptibility only in part: scored naively it appears near 0.9, largely because catalogued failures cluster in wetter ranges; matched against comparable nearby sites the honest figures are 0.76, 0.71, and 0.54 (no better than chance). The burst signal holds within single regions, reaching 0.89 in Nepal alone. Five deep-learning models do not decisively beat a simple gradient-boosted baseline. Three score marginally higher on landslides, a hint too small to confirm. For the lake hazards the baseline wins outright, reproduced by a three-rule decision tree on ruggedness and monsoon rainfall. We close with a ranked Nepal watchlist, a prioritisation aid, not a prediction, and note where free data reaches its limits.

</details>


### [89] [MARCH: Scaling Recurrent Memory with Content-Routed State Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12435)
*Ming Zhang,Kaisen Yang,Shu Yu,Ermo Hua,Ning Ding,Xia Hu,Bowen Zhou,Chaochao Lu,Youbang Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MARCH is a new neural architecture that combines transformer-like memory with recurrent efficiency by using state anchors and content-routed attention to scale beyond fixed-size dimensions while maintaining computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformers have strong long-context retrieval but suffer from quadratic training complexity and linear inference memory growth. Recurrent alternatives are computationally efficient but underperform on recall tasks because they overwrite earlier information and only retain recent context.

Method: MARCH periodically caches cumulative recurrent-state checkpoints as state anchors, each associated with a compact content-conditioned anchor key. This creates a memory bank that grows with context length. At each token, it produces an anchor query to attend to all causally available state anchors, with output calculated as attention-style aggregation over historical anchors along the current state.

Result: After standard pretraining, MARCH consistently outperforms multiple linear attention variants across commonsense reasoning, LongBench, and in-context retrieval benchmarks.

Conclusion: Content-routed state caching substantially strengthens recurrent long-range memory while preserving its native computation path, offering a controllable trade-off between historical resolution and memory cost.

Abstract: Transformers owe much of their strong long-context retrieval capability to a token-level memory that grows with context length. This flexibility, however, incurs a quadratic computation complexity during training and a key--value cache that grows linearly during autoregressive inference. Recurrent alternatives offer efficient decoding by compressing the entire history into a fixed-size state, but often underperform on recall-intensive tasks since earlier associations usually get overwritten by subsequent updates, and only the most recent contextual information is retained. In this paper, we introduce Memory-Anchor Routing across Context History (MARCH), a network architecture that effectively scales state-space models beyond a fixed-size dimension, while maintaining computational efficiency over long-sequences. MARCH periodically caches cumulative recurrent-state checkpoints as state anchors and associates each anchor with a compact, content-conditioned anchor key. This lets MARCH maintain a memory bank, which can grow as context length increases, providing a controllable trade-off between historical resolution and memory cost. At each token, MARCH produces an anchor query to attend all causally available state anchors, and the output is calculated as an attention-style aggregation over all historical anchors along the current state. We show that after standard pretraining, MARCH consistently outperforms multiple linear attention variants across commonsense reasoning, LongBench, and in-context retrieval. These results demonstrate that content-routed state caching substantially strengthens recurrent long-range memory while preserving its native computation path.

</details>


### [90] [Unifying Generative Models with Path Integrals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12438)
*Ramon Winterhalder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a unified framework for generative modeling as a path integral, showing how different models (flow-based, diffusion, variational, adversarial) emerge from different evaluation principles of a single master action, enabling diagrammatic perturbation theory and one-loop corrections to deterministic samplers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a unified theoretical framework that connects different generative modeling approaches (flow-based, diffusion-based, variational, adversarial) under a single formalism, enabling systematic analysis and improvements through field-theoretic methods like perturbation theory.

Method: Formulates generative modeling as a path integral using Martin-Siggia-Rose-Janssen-de Dominicis (MSRJD) formalism, which separates free from interacting probability flows and enables diagrammatic perturbation theory. The framework allows for systematic expansions including one-loop corrections to deterministic samplers.

Result: The unified framework shows different generative models arise from different evaluation principles of a single master action. The perturbation expansion yields a one-loop correction to deterministic samplers without stochastic-sampling cost, reducing a 53% tree-level error to 1.6% on nonlinear drifts. The approach also handles imperfect learned scores and enables symmetry-equivariant drift design with EFT power counting.

Conclusion: The path integral formulation provides a powerful unified framework for generative modeling that connects different approaches, enables systematic perturbation theory analysis, and yields practical improvements like efficient one-loop corrections to deterministic samplers while maintaining connections to field-theoretic methods.

Abstract: We formulate generative modeling as a path integral in which flow-based, diffusion-based, variational, and adversarial models arise as different evaluation principles for a single master action. Its Martin-Siggia-Rose-Janssen-de~Dominicis (MSRJD) form separates free from interacting probability flows and opens them to diagrammatic perturbation theory. The expansion yields a one-loop correction to deterministic samplers at no stochastic-sampling cost, which we validate on solvable and nonlinear drifts, where it reduces a 53 % tree-level error to 1.6 %. Imperfect learned scores enter as insertions and yield a response-weighted score-matching objective, and symmetry-equivariant drift design becomes an operator expansion with EFT power counting.

</details>


### [91] [Dual Spatial-Temporal Attribution: Architecture-Aligned Post-Hoc Explainability for Recurrent Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12441)
*Iyad Assaad Nekka,Hamida Seba,Khaled Walid Hidouci,Karima Amrouche*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: X-AddGraph is a post-hoc explainability framework for AddGraph (a GCN+GRU anomaly detector for dynamic graphs) that provides edge-level explanations using a Dual Spatial-Temporal Attribution mechanism without changing detection performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning anomaly detectors for dynamic graphs like AddGraph are accurate but opaque - they provide anomaly scores without explanations, which is problematic for regulated systems where decisions need to be auditable and trustworthy. AddGraph has never been equipped with any explainability despite being a foundational framework.

Method: X-AddGraph is a strictly post-hoc explainability framework using Dual Spatial-Temporal Attribution (DSTA) with three components aligned with AddGraph's architecture: 1) gradient-based relevance attribution over current adjacency structure (spatial), 2) direct reading of contextual attention weights computed during inference (short-term temporal), 3) gradient rollback through recurrent hidden states (long-term temporal). The detector remains frozen to preserve performance.

Result: On UCI Message benchmark, AddGraph baseline reaches average per-snapshot AUC of 0.8705 (exceeding original published result). X-AddGraph reproduces identical detection scores while adding explanations. Long-term attribution identifies historical snapshots with significantly more counterfactual signal (0.127 vs 0.074 random) than spatially-blind explainers, particularly useful for analyzing different edge populations (true positives, false positives, etc.).

Conclusion: X-AddGraph successfully adds explainability to AddGraph without compromising detection performance, providing both spatial and temporal attribution for anomaly explanations in dynamic graphs. The framework enables trustworthy auditing of automated anomaly detection decisions in regulated systems.

Abstract: Deep learning detectors for anomalies in dynamic graphs have reached strong accuracy, yet they remain opaque: when an edge is flagged, the analyst receives a score but no reason. This opacity is untenable in the cooperative, regulated information systems where such detectors are deployed, where automated decisions must be auditable and trustworthy. We address this gap for AddGraph, the foundational GCN+GRU framework for edge-level anomaly detection in dynamic graphs, which to our knowledge has never been equipped with any form of explainability. We present a strictly post-hoc explainability framework, X-AddGraph, built on a Dual Spatial-Temporal Attribution (DSTA) mechanism whose three components are each aligned with one of AddGraph's architectural modules: a gradient-based relevance attribution over the current adjacency structure (spatial), a direct reading of the contextual attention weights already computed during inference (short-term temporal, at zero additional cost), and a gradient rollback through the recurrent hidden states (long-term temporal). Because the detector is frozen, detection performance is preserved exactly (Delta AUC = 0, verified empirically to ten decimal places). On the UCI Message benchmark, our trained AddGraph baseline reaches an average per-snapshot AUC of 0.8705, exceeding the originally published result; X-AddGraph reproduces every score identically while adding explanations where none existed. Evaluated across four edge populations - confident true positives, low-confidence true positives, false positives, and random samples - the long-term attribution identifies historical snapshots carrying significantly more counterfactual signal than random selection (0.127 vs. 0.074), a capability that no spatially-blind explainer can provide. We release our implementation for full reproducibility.

</details>


### [92] [Personalized Scorer Modeling: A Learning-Based Framework for Deriving Robust Sleep Stage Labels from Multiple Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12446)
*Seyyed Ali Hoseini,Javad Baseri,Hamid Saadatfar,Edris Hoseini Gol,AmirHossein Eshghi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learning-based hypnogram method that aggregates multiple scorers' labels using confusion matrix modeling to create more reliable sleep stage references, outperforming single-reference approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most automatic sleep stage classification studies evaluate models against a single reference hypnogram despite known inter-scorer variability, which limits reliability and robustness of the evaluation.

Method: Proposed Learning-Based Hypnogram (LBH) that models each scorer's stage-specific behavior using confusion matrices derived from machine learning models. After column normalization, these matrices estimate probability of true sleep stage given scorer's label, then aggregate probabilities across scorers to assign final labels. Evaluated with random forest, SVM, and MLP classifiers on EEG-only and EEG+EMG features from DOD-H and DOD-O datasets.

Result: LBH consistently improved overall performance compared to dataset hypnogram (DH) and best-scorer hypnogram (BSH). Best results with random forest and EEG+EMG: 86.07% accuracy, 85.46% precision, 85.29% F1-score on DOD-H; 86.04% accuracy, 85.21% precision, 84.70% F1-score on DOD-O.

Conclusion: Personalized scorer modeling can improve reference hypnogram construction without discarding information from individual experts, providing more reliable sleep stage labels by leveraging collective behavior of multiple scorers.

Abstract: Sleep stage classification is important for the diagnosis and management of sleep disorders, yet most automatic staging studies evaluate models against a single reference hypnogram despite known inter-scorer variability. This study investigates whether multi-scored datasets can be used to construct more reliable reference labels from the collective behavior of multiple experts. We use the publicly available DOD-H and DOD-O datasets. EEG (C3-M2) and chin EMG signals were segmented into 30-s epochs, and 30 features were extracted from each modality, yielding 60 features for EEG+EMG. We propose a learning-based hypnogram (LBH) that models the stage-specific behavior of each scorer using confusion matrices derived from machine-learning models. After column normalization, these matrices estimate the probability of each true sleep stage given each scorer's label; probabilities are aggregated across scorers to assign the final label for each epoch. LBH was evaluated with random forest, support vector machine, and multilayer perceptron classifiers under EEG-only and EEG+EMG settings, and compared with the dataset hypnogram (DH) and best-scorer hypnogram (BSH). LBH consistently improved overall performance. The best results were obtained with random forest and EEG+EMG, reaching 86.07% accuracy, 85.46% precision, and 85.29% F1-score on DOD-H, and 86.04% accuracy, 85.21% precision, and 84.70% F1-score on DOD-O. These findings suggest that personalized scorer modeling can improve reference hypnogram construction without discarding information from individual experts.

</details>


### [93] [Geometric and Behavioral Stratification in Transformer Residual Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12447)
*Nelson Guda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The prediction direction (unembedding direction of the model's current token prediction) serves as a content-defined privileged anchor that organizes residual stream geometry, with prediction-proximal regions being highly structured and functionally decisive while distal regions expand with model scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While transformer models are known to develop privileged bases with different statistical properties, it's unclear what specific direction these bases select. The researchers aim to understand the nature and significance of the prediction direction as a geometric organizer within the residual stream.

Method: Investigating the prediction direction across 18 different transformer models (dense and mixture-of-experts architectures, ranging from 7B to 120B parameters, both base and instruction-tuned versions). Analyzing residual stream variation relative to this anchor through geometric measurements, studying stratification patterns, and conducting disruption experiments to understand causal relationships.

Result: The prediction direction functions as a privileged anchor that stratifies residual stream variation: 1) Prediction-proximal regions are narrow, highly structured, and cluster related prompts, while distal regions expand with model scale, 2) This organization is partially recovered by variance-based analyses but becomes less effective with prompt heterogeneity, 3) Disrupting variance directions closest to the prediction causes immediate divergence and task-frame shifts, 4) The complement is weakly aligned yet causally load-bearing, with behavior driven by direction rather than magnitude.

Conclusion: The prediction direction is a distinct privileged anchor that provides a geometric explanation for how high-dimensional computation coexists with linear readout in transformer models, revealing a stratified organization where narrow prediction-proximal interfaces concentrate readout-relevant structure while complementary regions scale with model capacity.

Abstract: Trained transformer models develop privileged bases: coordinate axes whose statistics differ from the rest of the residual stream. But what kind of direction does such a basis select? We investigate the prediction direction, the unembedding direction of the token a model currently predicts, and find that it functions as a content-defined privileged anchor. Measured with respect to this anchor, residual-stream variation is geometrically and behaviorally stratified by proximity to the prediction.
  The stratification holds in all eighteen models tested (dense and mixture-of-experts, 7B-120B, base and instruction-tuned). A narrow, scale-invariant prediction interface concentrates readout-relevant structure, while the vast prediction-distal complement expands with model scale. Because the prediction direction sits nearly orthogonal to the principal variance axes, variance-based analyses recover this organization only partly, and the shortfall grows with prompt heterogeneity.
  Anchoring reveals a steep geometric gradient: prediction-proximal regions are highly structured and cluster related prompts, while the complement is flatter and anti-discriminates among prompt groups. The interface is a narrow slice but functionally decisive. Disrupting the variance directions closest to the prediction causes immediate divergence and frequent task-frame shifts; disrupting the next level down delays divergence and preserves framing. The complement is weakly readout-aligned per direction yet causally and temporally load-bearing, and behavior is driven by direction rather than magnitude.
  These results establish the prediction direction as a privileged anchor distinct from previously described coordinate axes, and give a geometric account of how high-dimensional computation coexists with linear readout.

</details>


### [94] [Exemplar-based objective classification of gust-induced loads across multiple flight conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12448)
*Paolo Olivucci,Kowshik Srivatsan,David E. Rival*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Using ML representation and exemplar selection to objectively classify gust-induced loads across flight conditions, revealing nine recurring fundamental response types that provide physical insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To find an objective classification criterion for gust-induced loads that remains interpretable while organizing complexity across many flight conditions, avoiding coarse parameter-based labels like flight attitude alone.

Method: Encode experimental observations through machine-learned representation, apply summarization procedure to select minimal subset of highly significant exemplars, then use these exemplars as similarity-based objective classification criterion.

Result: Applied to database of 3480 pressure-load measurements from random gusts on flying-wing model across six flight attitudes, identified nine fundamental response types recurring across multiple attitudes, enabling physical intuition into underlying fluid mechanics through transient response analysis.

Conclusion: The approach successfully provides objective classification of complex gust-induced loads while maintaining interpretability, revealing fundamental physical response patterns that transcend simple flight attitude categorization and offer insights into fluid mechanics.

Abstract: Is it possible to find an objective classification criterion that organizes the complexity of gust-induced loads across many flight conditions? And one that remains as interpretable as a labelling based on coarse parameters, such as the flight attitude? Our approach encodes a large number of experimental observations through a machine-learned representation and applies a summarization procedure to select a minimal subset of highly significant exemplars. The exemplars provide a similarity-based objective classification criterion of all the observations, they can be more conveniently inspected by experts and can become subject of more refined experiments. We demonstrate the approach on a database of 3480 pressure-load measurements induced by random gusts on a flying-wing model across six flight attitudes. We find nine fundamental response types that recur across multiple attitudes; analysis of a type's transient response enables physical intuition into the underlying fluid mechanics.

</details>


### [95] [Learning Under Treatment-Induced Label Indeterminacy with Expert Annotations of Counterfactual Outcomes: A Case Study in Neurological Prognostication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12477)
*Xiaobin Shen,Chloe Y. H. Huang,Jonathan Elmer,George H. Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: When clinical outcomes become unobservable due to treatment decisions, standard prediction model evaluation fails. Proposed framework separates evaluation for patients with observed vs. uncertain outcomes, revealing tradeoffs between accuracy on certain cases and alignment with expert counterfactual estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical prediction models typically assume cleanly observed outcomes, but this fails when treatment decisions make clinically relevant outcomes permanently unobservable. This is particularly problematic in neurological prognostication after cardiac arrest, where treatment withdrawal decisions create patients with indeterminate outcomes.

Method: Proposed framework splits evaluation between "certain cases" (patients with observed outcomes) and "uncertain cases" (patients with indeterminate outcomes due to treatment decisions). Uses a cohort of 2,497 cardiac arrest patients, with 1,429 having uncertain outcomes reviewed by clinical experts who provided counterfactual outcome estimates. Developed a neural model using both types of labels with adjustable tradeoff between them.

Result: Models with similar AUROC on certain cases show substantial differences in Brier scores and probability estimates for uncertain cases. Improving alignment with expert counterfactual estimates for uncertain cases generally worsens accuracy on certain cases, revealing a tradeoff that standard evaluation misses.

Conclusion: When treatment decisions determine outcome observability, conventional metrics can miss important failure modes in the very patients who need prognostic support most. The proposed evaluation framework highlights crucial tradeoffs between accuracy on observed cases and alignment with expert judgment on uncertain cases.

Abstract: Clinical prediction models are often developed as if the outcome of interest were cleanly observed for every patient. This assumption fails when treatment decisions make the clinically relevant outcome permanently unobservable. As a case study of this problem, we consider post-cardiac-arrest neurological prognostication using a cohort of 2,497 patients, including 1,429 patients whose outcomes were rendered indeterminate by treatment decisions. These patients with indeterminate outcomes were reviewed by independent clinical experts, who provided their guesses of counterfactual outcomes about what would have happened to the patients. We refer to these patients as uncertain cases. We also have patients for whom we observe their clinically relevant outcomes; we refer to these patients as certain cases. We propose a framework for evaluating prediction models that explicitly splits the evaluation between certain and uncertain cases. Here, we cannot easily evaluate both types of cases in a uniform manner as the available target labels differ. We then propose a simple prediction model that uses target labels from both certain and uncertain cases in a manner that allows us to trade off between them. Across the proposed neural model and a collection of tabular baselines, models with similar certain-case AUROC can nevertheless differ substantially in both certain-case Brier score and their probability estimates for uncertain cases. Improving alignment with target labels of uncertain cases for our proposed model generally comes at the cost of worse accuracy on certain cases, highlighting an explicit tradeoff that standard evaluation conceals. These results show that when treatment decisions determine whether clinically meaningful outcomes remain observable, conventional evaluation metrics can miss important failure modes in the very patients for whom prognostic support matters most.

</details>


### [96] [When Can You Trust Offline Evaluation of Equal-Cost Top-k Allocation? A Controlled, Reproducible Benchmark and Practitioner's Guide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12489)
*Binshuang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper benchmarks off-policy evaluation methods for deterministic top-k targeting rules under budget constraints, revealing critical issues with weak overlap, propensity estimation errors, and the optimizer's curse that persist despite standard fixes like cross-fitting and bounded weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations need to evaluate deterministic top-k targeting rules (who to treat under budget constraints) before deployment using logged data. However, off-policy evaluation for deterministic policies faces unique challenges: weak overlap hits estimates directly without averaging, and standard methods may fail in practical settings.

Method: Benchmarked six estimators across five datasets with two known-effect sweeps, validated against non-simulated paired references. Used synthetic and semi-synthetic exact-value surfaces with propensities floored at 0.02. Analyzed weak overlap mechanisms through logger-target action alignment, effective sample size diagnostics, and propensity estimation error impacts.

Result: 1) Weak overlap is governed by logger-target action alignment, not logging sharpness alone. 2) Cross-fitting outcome nuisances doesn't fix the optimizer's curse - honest policy-level splitting is needed. 3) Propensity-estimation error is the largest degradation factor, hurting IPS significantly while leaving doubly-robust methods almost unchanged. 4) The propensity floor reduces tuned hybrids to untuned parents, leaving four practically distinct estimators.

Conclusion: Standard off-policy evaluation methods have serious limitations for deterministic top-k targeting rules. Key failures persist despite bounded weights and common fixes. Practitioners need different diagnostics (action alignment over logging sharpness), honest policy-level splitting instead of just cross-fitting, and should be aware of how propensity estimation errors degrade different estimators unequally. The benchmark (using public data only) provides tools for better evaluation.

Abstract: Organizations decide whom to treat under a budget and want to know what a targeting rule would have earned before deploying it. Off-policy evaluation promises this from logged data, but the deployable rule is a deterministic top-k policy: it removes all averaging over actions, so weak overlap hits the estimate directly. We benchmark six estimators across five datasets and two known-effect sweeps, and validate the mechanisms against a non-simulated paired reference. First, weak overlap is governed by logger-target action alignment, not by logging sharpness alone: what governs support is the logger's probability of the target's actions. Sharpening a logger built from the target's own score barely moves overlap over the tested range; action-level disagreement collapses it. Effective sample size ranks this risk across logging environments, but is weak at ranking candidates within the single log a practitioner holds, and its cut point does not transfer. Second, the optimizer's curse is not fixed by cross-fitting the outcome nuisance. When the rule is fit on the data used to evaluate it, cross-fitting the nuisance alone leaves the reuse bias in place and makes it worse. Honest policy-level splitting avoids the reuse by targeting the learning procedure's value -- a change of estimand, not a de-biasing of the full-sample policy. Third, propensity-estimation error is the largest degradation we measure: an out-of-fold estimate hurts IPS more than any other stress we apply, leaves doubly-robust estimation almost unchanged, and can invert the overlap diagnostic itself. Logging is synthesized and propensities floored at 0.02, so every failure occurs with bounded weights; the floor also reduces the two tuned hybrids to their untuned parents, leaving four practically distinct estimators, and all exact-value surfaces are synthetic or semi-synthetic. We release the benchmark; public data only.

</details>


### [97] [Exploring Oversmoothing with Householder Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12514)
*Bhaskar Karol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HouseGNN introduces a novel graph neural network that uses Householder reflectors to prevent oversmoothing by preserving node-wise Euclidean norms across deep layers, maintaining orthogonal transformations to combat representation collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep graph neural networks suffer from oversmoothing - the progressive collapse of node representations toward a low information subspace as network depth increases. This occurs because normalized graph propagation operators are repeatedly applied directly to hidden representations, causing information loss.

Method: HouseGNN uses aggregated neighborhood messages not to update hidden states directly, but to estimate reflection directions. The node embedding is then updated by a Householder reflector followed by GroupSort, creating piecewise orthogonal layers that preserve Euclidean norm at every node and depth.

Result: The authors prove three core properties: (1) every internal layer preserves node-wise Euclidean norm, (2) the Householder reflector is scale and sign-invariant in the message, and (3) pairwise distances between nodes can change through mismatches between node-wise orthogonal operators, allowing for meaningful representation updates.

Conclusion: HouseGNN provides a theoretically-grounded solution to oversmoothing in deep GNNs by employing orthogonal transformations through Householder reflectors, enabling the construction of deeper graph neural networks without representation collapse while maintaining useful distance metrics between nodes.

Abstract: Deep graph neural networks(GNNs) suffer from oversmoothing- a progressive collapse of node representation towards a low information subspace as network depth increases because the normalized graph propagation operator is repeatedly applied directly to the hidden representations. In this work we study Householder Graph Neural Network (HouseGNN). Rather than updating the hidden state like standard GCN, HouseGNN uses the aggregated neighbourhood message solely to estimate a reflection direction; the node embedding is then updated by a Householder reflector followed by GroupSort, yielding a piecewise orthogonal layer that preserves Euclidean norm at every node and at every depth. We prove three core properties: (i) every internal layer preserves the node-wise Euclidean norm; (ii) the Householder reflector is scale scale and sign-invariant in the message; and (iii) pairwise distance between nodes can change through mismatch between node-wise orthogonal operators.

</details>


### [98] [GENADA: efficient generative time series adversarial attack framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12535)
*Michael Baronov,Denis Vorobev,Margarita Rusanova,Petr Sokerin,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GENADA is a generative adversarial attack method that uses a learned generative model to create deceptive perturbations for time series models in a single forward pass, achieving comparable attack quality to iterative gradient methods with much faster inference time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for time series analysis are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, but traditional gradient-based attacks are computationally expensive because they require iterative backpropagation through the victim model to compute input gradients during multiple refinement steps.

Method: The authors propose GENADA (GENerative ADversarial Attack), which learns a generative model to produce deceptive perturbations directly in a single forward pass. They develop a training procedure for this generative model, with variants including both single-step and iterative generative attack schemes.

Result: GENADA was validated by attacking several neural models and datasets in the time-series domain, including a controlled low-dimensional setting.
