Method: The model represents each patient with a structured latent state initialized from baseline imaging (3D spatial latent state). It then updates this state through time-ordered post-intervention events using procedural context, static covariates, elapsed time, and peri-event physiological embeddings. Follow-up imaging provides training-only supervision through a latent forecasting objective.

Result: Applied to atrial fibrillation ablation, the model achieves AUROC 0.756 and AUPRC 0.777 for recurrence prediction on DECAAF-II dataset during 90-day recovery window. It also achieves scar-extent MAE of 2.971 percentage points without requiring follow-up MRI intensities at inference. The learned state supports recurrence-risk queries at different horizons and retrospective input editing.

Conclusion: The intervention-aware clinical world model effectively captures the irregular trajectory of post-intervention recovery, leveraging time-ordered clinical events to provide clinically meaningful risk assessment and prediction of long-term outcomes, demonstrating significant potential for improving clinical prediction in complex procedural contexts.

Abstract: Many clinical prediction models treat post-intervention outcomes as a one-step mapping from baseline measurements to a future endpoint. However, recovery after a procedure often unfolds as an irregular trajectory: clinical observations, medication changes, repeat interventions, and physiological measurements are recorded asynchronously and can change risk assessment over time. We propose an intervention-aware clinical world model that represents each patient with a structured latent state and evolves it through time-ordered post-intervention events. The model first encodes baseline imaging into a 3D spatial latent state. It then updates this state using procedural context, static covariates, elapsed time, and peri-event physiological embeddings. Follow-up imaging provides training-only supervision through a latent forecasting objective. We apply the framework to atrial fibrillation ablation. During the 90-day recovery window, irregular post-procedure records provide clinically meaningful evidence for long-term recurrence risk. In repeated internal cross-validation on DECAAF-II, our model achieves AUROC 0.756 and AUPRC 0.777 for recurrence prediction. It also achieves a scar-extent MAE of 2.971 percentage points without requiring follow-up MRI intensities at inference. The learned state supports recurrence-risk queries at different horizons and retrospective input editing of blanking-period records.

</details>


### [173] [The data geometry of masking diffusion: Certified-optimal schedules via unmasking growth complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13520)
*Martin J. Wainwright*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Unmasking Growth Complexity (UGC) metric to analyze discrete diffusion sampling, develops optimal sampling schedules based on UGC, and shows certified-optimal samplers that achieve specified error bounds with near-oracle complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing discrete diffusion methods lack geometric understanding of data structure during unmasking processes, leading to suboptimal sampling schedules and inefficient computational allocation.

Method: Introduces Unmasking Growth Complexity (UGC) to measure data geometry during unmasking. Uses log-reveal-odds coordinates to optimize single-block and multi-block sampling schedules. Shows how UGC increments can be estimated from samples via KL increments along coupled reveal trajectories.

Result: Develops certified-optimal samplers achieving prescribed KL error with high probability and iteration complexity within constant factor of oracle procedures. Shows UGC connects to classical multivariate dependence measures and complexity measures. Demonstrates substantial dimension-dependent gains, including √d improvements with constant number of adaptive blocks.

Conclusion: UGC provides unified framework for analyzing discrete diffusion, enabling geometry-aware sampling schedules that adapt computational effort to data structure, with significant theoretical and practical advantages over coarse schedules.

Abstract: We study masking diffusion for discrete sampling and introduce a path-resolved measure of data geometry called the \emph{unmasking growth complexity} ({\textsf{UGC}\xspace}). Its local increments directly control Kullback--Leibler (KL) discretization error, yielding a unified analysis of Bernoulli-subset and fixed-cardinality unmasking schemes. In log-reveal-odds coordinates, this structure yields optimized single-block and multi-block schedules, and quantifies the gains from adapting computational effort to data geometry. Crucially, we show how {\textsf{UGC}\xspace} increments can be estimated from samples via KL increments along coupled reveal trajectories. This leads to \emph{certified-optimal} samplers that achieve a prescribed KL error with high probability and iteration complexity within a constant factor of the corresponding oracle procedure. Collapsing the \ugc path yields the aggregate {\textsf{UGC}\xspace} mass, which connects to classical multivariate dependence measures and complexity measures from previous analyses of discrete diffusion. In the fine-partition limit, the squared integral of the square-root {\textsf{UGC}\xspace} density determines the sharp leading-order optimal Euler discretization error. Examples exhibit substantial dimension-dependent gains over coarse schedules, including $\widetildeΩ(\sqrt{d})$ improvements achievable with a constant number of adaptively placed blocks.

</details>


### [174] [Vero: Can AI Agents Build Formally Verified Software Repositories?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13522)
*Zhe Ye,Hantao Lou,Yuechun Sun,Peiyang Song,Zhengxu Yan,Timothe Kasriel,Qingyang Zhang,Kaiyu Yang,Soonho Kong,Jingxuan He,Dawn Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vero is the first benchmark for evaluating joint implementation and proof synthesis at the repository level, covering 43 multi-module instances from real-world repositories across multiple languages and domains, showing that current AI agents can only fully solve 27/43 instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AI agents are increasingly used for programming, they don't guarantee correctness of generated code. Verified code generation (producing both implementation and proof) offers trustworthy AI-generated software, but existing benchmarks either focus on individual functions or evaluate proof generation with provided implementations only. It's unknown whether agents can make coherent implementation and proof choices across real multi-module codebases.

Method: Created Vero benchmark containing 43 multi-module instances sourced from real-world repositories spanning Python, Dafny, Verus, and Coq, covering domains like cryptographic protocols and distributed systems. Each instance is a multi-module Lean 4 repository with predetermined API interfaces, manually curated formal specifications, and reference implementations. Includes audit mechanism allowing agents to prove unsatisfiability of specifications or incorrectness of reference code to correct latent errors.

Result: Evaluated frontier coding-agent configurations with Lean toolchain access. The strongest agent fully solved only 27 of 43 instances and closed no specifications on the hardest repositories. Vero surfaces gaps in current agents' ability to handle repository-scale verified software synthesis.

Conclusion: Vero provides a concrete testbed for measuring progress toward repository-scale verified software synthesis where current agents still fall short, demonstrating the need for more advanced AI systems capable of coherent implementation and proof synthesis across multi-module codebases.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly used for programming, but do not provide any guarantee on the correctness of generated code. Verified code generation, in which an agent produces both an implementation and a machine-checked proof of its specification, offers a stronger path toward trustworthy AI-generated software. Existing benchmarks in this direction either focus on individual functions or only evaluate proof generation with provided implementations. It is still an open question whether agents can make coherent implementation and proof choices across real multi-module codebases. To bridge this gap, we introduce Vero, the first benchmark to evaluate joint implementation and proof synthesis at the repository level. Vero contains 43 multi-module instances sourced from real-world repositories spanning Python, Dafny, Verus, and Coq, and covering diverse domains from cryptographic protocols to distributed systems. Each instance consists of a multi-module Lean 4 repository with predetermined API interfaces, manually curated formal specifications, and reference implementations, supporting both proof-only and code-and-proof evaluation modes. To improve benchmark reliability, Vero also includes an audit mechanism where agents are allowed to formally prove unsatisfiability of provided specification or incorrectness of reference code, which surfaces and corrects latent code and specification errors during curation. We evaluate frontier coding-agent configurations with Lean toolchain access. The strongest agent fully solves only 27 of 43 instances and closes no specifications on the hardest repositories. Vero provides a concrete testbed for measuring progress toward repository-scale verified software synthesis, where current agents still fall short. We release the benchmark, curation pipeline, and evaluation harness at https://github.com/sunblaze-ucb/vero.

</details>


### [175] [DARTree: Speculative Diffusion Decoding with Autoregressive Draft Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13524)
*Tianyi Li,Yaxin Luo,Xinyi Shang,Zhiqiang Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DARTree is a training-free speculative decoding method that extends autoregressive correction heads from chains to trees, enabling parallel draft verification with improved candidate coverage while maintaining best-first tree pruning for efficient verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based drafters predict token blocks in parallel but use marginal distributions rather than conditioned token probabilities along draft paths. Current recurrent correction only incorporates causal information along single chains, and while diffusion-based tree construction broadens candidate coverage, it doesn't carry correction along individual branches, limiting speculative decoding efficiency.

Method: DARTree constructs a fixed-width candidate tree by expanding and scoring all nodes at each depth in a single batch, then applies best-first pruning to select the verification tree. This decouples autoregressive correction head inference from sequential heap operations, extending pretrained AR correction heads from chains to trees without additional training.

Result: Across seven math, code, and chat benchmarks, DARTree achieves the highest average acceptance length and speedup in all four model-temperature configurations. It accepts up to 12.97 tokens per verification round (98.6% more than DFlash and 27.9% more than Domino) and reaches up to 9.73× lossless speedup over locally measured autoregressive decoding.

Conclusion: DARTree successfully enables training-free extension of autoregressive correction heads to tree structures, significantly improving speculative decoding efficiency through better candidate coverage while maintaining efficient verification via best-first pruning, achieving state-of-the-art performance across diverse benchmarks.

Abstract: Speculative decoding losslessly accelerates autoregressive language models by verifying multiple draft tokens in parallel. Diffusion-based drafters further reduce proposal latency by predicting an entire token block in parallel, but their position-wise distributions are marginal rather than conditioned on tokens selected along each draft path. Existing recurrent correction incorporates causal information along a single draft chain, whereas diffusion-based tree construction broadens candidate coverage without carrying this correction along individual branches. We introduce DARTree, a training-free speculative decoding method that extends a pretrained AR correction head from chains to trees. DARTree first constructs a fixed-width candidate tree by expanding and scoring all nodes at each depth in a single batch, and then only applies best-first pruning to select the verification tree, decoupling AR-head inference from sequential heap operations. Across seven math, code, and chat benchmarks, DARTree achieves the highest average acceptance length and speedup in all four model--temperature configurations, accepting up to 12.97 tokens per verification round, 98.6\% more than DFlash and 27.9\% more than Domino in the same setting, and reaching up to 9.73$\times$ lossless speedup over locally measured autoregressive decoding.

</details>


### [176] [Exponential Convex Calibration Dimension for the Multi-Label Jaccard Measure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13549)
*Mingyuan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves that exact calibration for Jaccard score/IoU in multi-label classification requires exponentially many prediction dimensions, but provides polynomial-dimensional approximations with explicit regret bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Jaccard score/IoU is standard in multi-label classification and segmentation, but its loss matrix has exponential size (2^s outcomes). Understanding the calibration dimension and finding efficient surrogates with provable guarantees is needed for practical applications.

Method: 1) Proved Jaccard score matrices are nonsingular with affine dimension 2^s-1 using finite MinHash Gram representation and Boolean Möbius inversion. 2) Derived exact calibration dimension bounds. 3) Developed polynomial-time approximation surrogates: an F1-to-Jaccard transfer rule and MinHash square-loss surrogates with explicit dimension and regret bounds.

Result: 1) Lower bound: CCdim(L^Jac) ≥ 2^{s-1} (requires exponential dimension for exact calibration). 2) Upper bound: CCdim(L^Jac) ≤ 2^s-1. 3) Polynomial approximations: (s^2+1)-dimensional F1 surrogate gives Jaccard regret ≤ 3-2√2; MinHash surrogates achieve any fixed additive regret α with dimensions O(s^2/α^2) or O(s/α^2) depending on variant.

Conclusion: Exact calibration for Jaccard score requires exponential prediction dimension, making it impractical. However, polynomial-dimensional surrogates can achieve any fixed additive regret tolerance with provable guarantees, making approximate calibration feasible for real-world multi-label classification tasks.

Abstract: The per-instance Jaccard score, or intersection over union (IoU), is standard in multi-label classification and binary segmentation. With $s$ labels, its loss matrix has $2^s$ outcomes and reports. Under the convention $\mathrm{Jac}(\varnothing,\varnothing)=1$, we prove that the Jaccard score, shifted-loss, and ordinary loss matrices are nonsingular and that the loss columns have affine dimension $2^s-1$. The proof combines a finite MinHash Gram representation with Boolean Möbius inversion. For exact calibration, we prove $2^{s-1} \leq \mathrm{CCdim}(L^{\mathrm{Jac}}) \leq 2^s-1$. The lower bound uses a factorially weighted distribution with $2^{s-1}+1$ supported outcomes and Bayes-optimal reports. Consequently, every exactly calibrated convex surrogate requires exponentially many prediction coordinates. We also give two polynomial-dimensional approximation guarantees with explicit regret transfers. A new $F_1$-to-Jaccard transfer turns an existing $(s^2+1)$-dimensional $F_1$ surrogate into a polynomial-time rule with asymptotic Jaccard regret at most $3-2\sqrt{2}$. For any $α>0$ and $0<ρ<1$, a MinHash square-loss surrogate attains Jaccard-regret floor $α$ uniformly over arbitrary conditional label distributions. With probability at least $1-ρ$, the direct construction has dimension $O((s^2+s\log(1/ρ))/α^2)$, while a signed variant has dimension $O((s+\log(1/ρ))/α^2)$. Thus zero-regret calibration requires exponential dimension, whereas every fixed additive regret tolerance admits polynomial prediction dimension.

</details>


### [177] [Defensive Boosting for Online Probabilistic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13554)
*Georgy Noarov,Aaron Roth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A defensive forecasting algorithm that combines both online gradient boosting and weak-to-strong boosting guarantees, achieving competitive Brier scores and classification error with efficient single weak-learner access.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing online boosting techniques provide separate incomparable guarantees: online gradient boosting competes in Brier score with best predictors in span of H, but fails when span lacks accurate predictors; weak-to-strong boosting drives classification error to zero under weak-learning condition, but offers little when condition fails. Need unified approach.

Method: Defensive Booster algorithm operationalizes the "dual view" of boosting: when randomized classification error is persistently high, mistake weights form smooth reweighting where every weak hypothesis has low edge, providing ex-post hard-core certificate that weak-learning condition fails. Uses defensive forecasting approach.

Result: Algorithm achieves both guarantees simultaneously: competitive Brier score with best predictor in span of H (same rate as online gradient boosting) AND when weak-learning condition satisfied, Brier score and classification error match online classification boosting rates. Strongly adaptive variant works on every time interval. Much faster runtime than ensemble methods.

Conclusion: Defensive Booster provides efficient unified solution to online probabilistic forecasting, achieving both gradient boosting and classification boosting guarantees with single weak-learner access, outperforming prior methods in predictive performance and runtime on synthetic and real data.

Abstract: We study online probabilistic forecasting of binary outcomes chosen by an adaptive adversary. Given an online learning algorithm for a weak hypothesis class $H$, we would like to efficiently obtain two incomparable guarantees that existing online boosting techniques provide separately. Online gradient boosting competes in Brier score with the best predictor induced by the span of $H$ on every sequence, but promises nothing when the span does not contain an accurate predictor. Online weak-to-strong boosting drives classification error to zero under a weak-learning condition, but promises little when that condition fails.
  We give a simple defensive forecasting algorithm, the Defensive Booster, that obtains both guarantees. On every adaptive sequence, its Brier score is competitive with the best prediction induced by the span of $H$ at the same rate as online gradient boosting; simultaneously, whenever the realized transcript satisfies the smooth weak-learning condition, its Brier score and randomized classification error satisfy the same rate guarantee as online classification boosting. This is achieved by operationalizing the "dual view" of boosting: When the algorithm's randomized classification error is persistently high, its mistake weights form a smooth reweighting on which every weak hypothesis has low edge, yielding an ex-post hard-core certificate that the weak-learning condition fails. We also develop a strongly adaptive variant, which satisfies both guarantees on every time interval. The Defensive Booster is very efficient: it accesses just one weak-class learner, whereas the prior online boosting methods we compare against maintain large weak-learner ensembles. Experiments on synthetic and real data streams demonstrate its strong predictive performance (sometimes substantially improving over all prior baselines) coupled with orders-of-magnitude faster runtime.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [178] [Entropy-Augmented Multi-Objective Policy Optimization in Multiagent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12534)
*Jamie Santos,Ayhan Alp Aydeniz,Raghav Thakar,Kagan Tumer*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper introduces an entropy-augmented policy evaluation strategy that adds a behavioral diversity signal to multi-objective evolutionary algorithms to prevent behavioral homogeneity in multiagent teams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard multi-objective evolutionary algorithms like NSGA-II focus on diversity in objective space but neglect behavioral diversity, which can lead to premature convergence and lack of adaptability to different environmental conditions in autonomous agent teams operating in complex environments like marine or extraterrestrial outposts.

Method: The researchers introduce an entropy-augmented policy evaluation strategy that incorporates an entropy bonus into agent fitness scores. This approach discourages behavioral homogeneity across the evolving population while preserving the underlying Pareto optimization framework of algorithms like NSGA-II.

Result: The method was evaluated across rover-domain experiments with qualitatively distinct reward structures and achieved hypervolume improvements of up to 48% relative to the NSGA-II baseline, demonstrating significant performance gains.

Conclusion: Behavioral diversity is a promising and underexplored direction for improving multi-objective multiagent evolutionary optimization, as incorporating behavioral diversity signals can lead to better exploration of distinct policies and improved overall performance.

Abstract: Autonomous agent teams deployed in settings such as marine and extraterrestrial outposts must coordinate actions to achieve optimal outcomes across multiple competing objectives. Multi-objective evolutionary algorithms such as NSGA-II optimize for diversity in the objective space, but neglect diversity in the behavior space, possibly leading to premature convergence and a collapse in behaviors that may differentiate policies in different external conditions. To address this, we introduce an entropy-augmented policy evaluation strategy that incorporates an entropy bonus into agent fitness scores, discouraging behavioral homogeneity across the evolving population. By augmenting policy evaluation with a behavior-space diversity signal while preserving the underlying Pareto optimization framework, our method is designed to encourage exploration of behaviorally distinct policies in multiagent domains. We evaluate our approach across rover-domain experiments with qualitatively distinct reward structures and observe hypervolume improvements of up to 48% relative to the NSGA-II baseline, suggesting that behavioral diversity is a promising and underexplored direction for improving multi-objective multiagent evolutionary optimization.

</details>


### [179] [Do LLMs Beat Nash? Testing Decentralized Coordination in Self-Play Multi-Agent Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12547)
*Deborah Sinishaw,Qile Zhu,Edwin Meriaux,Gregory Dudek*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Large language models can exceed game-theoretic Nash equilibrium benchmarks without communication, approaching optimal outcomes in some two-player games, but this capability degrades in larger teams with more actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLM agents can coordinate effectively without communication by reasoning about their counterparts, going beyond standard game-theoretic predictions for uncoordinated play.

Method: Benchmark of one-shot, no-communication games where each model knows only that counterparts run the same model. Evaluated 13 models against Nash equilibrium across two-player matrix games with 7 archetypes and 2-10 actions per player, plus team-based games with 4+ interchangeable agents.

Result: Two frontier-hosted models consistently exceed Nash benchmarks, approaching optimal joint outcomes in several archetypes. Most open-weight models achieve only partial gains varying by game structure. Performance degrades substantially in 4+ agent team games, especially with larger action spaces.

Conclusion: LLMs can reason effectively about counterparts in dyadic no-communication settings, but this coordination capability doesn't scale well to larger multi-agent teams, suggesting limitations in current models' reasoning about complex multi-agent interactions.

Abstract: Large language model agents deployed without a central controller are often assumed to require communication to coordinate their actions. We ask what remains possible without it: when independent instances of the same model cannot communicate, can they still reason about their counterparts well enough to exceed the standard game-theoretic baseline for uncoordinated play? We introduce a benchmark of one-shot, no-communication games in which each of thirteen language models is told only that its counterparts are running the same model and is evaluated against the Nash equilibrium of the underlying game. In two-player matrix games spanning seven archetypes and two to ten actions per player, two frontier-hosted models consistently exceed their Nash benchmark, approaching the optimal joint outcome in several archetypes, while most open-weight models achieve only partial gains that vary sharply by game structure. Performance degrades substantially in team-based games with four or more interchangeable agents, particularly as the action space grows, suggesting that whatever capability drives self-play gains in dyadic games does not transfer to larger multi-agent teams.

</details>


### [180] [Discovering Efficient and Explainable Communication Topologies for LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems via Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12921)
*Junzhi Li,Peng He,Qirui Ji,Wei Wang,Lixiang Liu,Chuxiong Sun*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: E2-Explainer is a framework for interpretable explanations of communication topologies in LLM-based multi-agent systems, identifying critical communication subgraphs via causal attribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing topology generation methods in LLM-based multi-agent systems use black-box optimization driven by task-level rewards, which provides little insight into why particular communication edges are selected and makes it difficult to identify critical communication subgraphs responsible for successful collaboration.

Method: Proposes E2-Explainer, a model-agnostic framework that formulates topology explanation as a causal attribution problem. It identifies compact communication subgraphs using edge-level evidence of task preservation via a Granger-style objective that measures how masking each communication channel changes task outcomes and response stability. These subgraphs are then distilled into an amortized explainer for efficient post-hoc explanation.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning and coding benchmarks show that E2-Explainer identifies critical communication subgraphs that preserve successful collaboration. These subgraphs can be executed directly to prune redundant communication edges, substantially reducing communication costs while maintaining competitive task performance.

Conclusion: E2-Explainer successfully addresses the interpretability gap in LLM-based multi-agent system communication topologies by providing causal, edge-level explanations that not only explain successful collaboration but also enable practical communication cost reduction through intelligent pruning.

Abstract: The performance of large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) largely depends on effective communication topologies. Existing topology generation methods, however, typically learn communication topologies through black-box optimization driven solely by task-level rewards. While effective, such optimization provides little insight into why particular communication edges are selected, making it difficult to identify the critical communication subgraphs responsible for successful collaboration. To address this limitation, we propose E2-Explainer, a model-agnostic framework for providing interpretable explanations of communication topologies produced by arbitrary topology generators. Specifically, we formulate topology explanation as a causal attribution problem that identifies compact communication subgraphs supported by edge-level evidence of task preservation. We obtain this evidence with a Granger-style objective that measures how masking each communication channel changes the task outcome and the stability of the final response. The resulting budgeted subgraphs are then distilled into an amortized explainer, enabling efficient post-hoc explanation without repeated edge-level evaluations at deployment. Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning and coding benchmarks demonstrate that E2-Explainer identifies critical communication subgraphs that preserve successful collaboration. These subgraphs can also be executed directly to prune redundant communication edges, substantially reducing communication costs while maintaining competitive task performance.

</details>


### [181] [Reconcile Once, Write Anytime: A Trust-Tiered Librarian and a Multi-Agent Writer for Drift-Free, Point-in-Time Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12984)
*Xing Zhang,Yanwei Cui,Guanghui Wang,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A two-tier agentic system separates knowledge management from report writing: a deterministic librarian builds a trust-tiered knowledge library with evidence cards and metric ledger, while a multi-agent writer composes contradiction-free reports at specific time cutoffs without data leakage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-form research reports generated by LLMs often contain contradictions, drift from facts, and lose provenance - metrics appear with different values, rumors are quoted as confidently as verified filings, and there's no clear temporal boundaries.

Method: Two-tier system: 1) Deterministic "librarian" ingests timestamped sources into trust-tiered ontology with evidence cards, metric ledger, and claim graph; 2) Portable multi-agent "writer" runtime composes reports at any cutoff T, reading only evidence with as_of <= T (no look-ahead). Red-team feedback flows back to librarian for self-correction.

Result: Evaluated on 6,130 sources (SEC filings, BLS data, Wikipedia) yielding 555,926 evidence cards. Shared metric ledger eliminated 6,845 contradictions. Tier-first selection achieved 22/22 accuracy vs 9/22 for popularity-first baseline. Zero media-sourced number leaks and no government stats displaced company filings. Red-team corrections propagated automatically. Zero look-ahead violations across 7 cutoffs. Difficulty-tiered routing exceeded quality ceiling while running 3.7x faster.

Conclusion: The two-tier system successfully addresses LLM report generation problems by separating knowledge management from writing, maintaining temporal consistency, eliminating contradictions through shared metric ledger, and enabling self-correction through red-team feedback loops.

Abstract: Long-form research reports generated by large language models drift, contradict themselves, and lose provenance: the same metric appears with different values, and rumor is quoted as confidently as an audited filing. We present a two-tier agentic system that separates a maintained, point-in-time knowledge library from report writing. A deterministic "librarian" ingests timestamped sources into a trust-tiered ontology, layering evidence cards, an authoritative metric ledger, and a claim graph into an always-current source of truth, not per-query RAG over raw chunks. A portable multi-agent "writer" runtime then composes a contradiction-free, evidence-grounded report at any knowledge cutoff T, reading only evidence with as_of <= T (no look-ahead); red-team verdicts flow back into the librarian. We evaluate on a self-collected, public corpus of 6,130 sources yielding 555,926 evidence cards (SEC EDGAR filings across 295 issuers and 11 sectors, U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics releases, and Wikipedia). From the one library we compose four point-in-time reports on distinct theses and run eight reproducible experiments, whose headline metrics come from a deterministic quality-control gate, itself validated by defect-injection meta-evaluation at recall 1.0 and precision 1.0. A shared metric ledger removes 6,845 cross-section contradictions to zero. Tier-first selection is correct on 22/22 gold cases where a popularity-first baseline scores only 9/22; trust tiering leaks zero media-sourced numbers, and no government statistic displaces a company's own filing. A red-team refutation propagates back and self-corrects a later run with zero manual edits. Replay exhibits zero look-ahead violations across seven cutoffs while the library grows from 235,373 to 555,312 cards. Difficulty-tiered model routing exceeds the all-Opus quality ceiling while running 3.7x faster than serial.

</details>
